 ### So what's the answer?

 That's a **lower bound, not a complete count**. The interesting next step would be to actually search the full released corpus for **AI, A.I., artificial intelligence, artificial general intelligence, AGI, machine intelligence, synthetic intelligence, intelligent machines, machine learning, robotics, etc.**, then deduplicate email threads and distinguish:

 - **Epstein personally talking about AI**
- **someone else talking to Epstein about AI**

[Oct 05, 2026, 4:09 PM EDT] RICKY:

why are you leaving shit out? wtf   other AI said this:

Yes. **Your list is substantially incomplete.** After cross-checking the corpus, I found several additional AI-related records and, importantly, some entries that should be separated into **direct correspondence, funding/proposal material, and merely forwarded/background material**.

 The biggest expansion is around **Ben Goertzel/OpenCog**: the released files contain multiple distinct proposals and research documents, not just the handful of emails you listed. The corpus search returns **62 documents containing the exact phrase “Artificial General Intelligence” in Datasets 9–11 alone**. That doesn't mean Epstein personally discussed AI in all 62, but it shows how much AI material surrounds his correspondence.  EFTASearch

 ### Additional things I'd add

 - **Ben Goertzel / OpenCog — multiple separate research proposals.**\
   There are substantial documents beyond the emails you listed. For example, the February 2013 proposal _Creating Robots with Toddler-Level Intelligence Using the OpenCog AGI Architecture_ explicitly proposed using OpenCog to produce toddler-level intelligence in a humanoid robot.  EFTASearch  \
   Another CogBot proposal describes integrating OpenCog with DeSTIN and using the system to control a humanoid robot.  EFTASearch
- **The CogPrime/OpenCog project.**\
   Another proposal describes developing general intelligence through the CogPrime architecture, including interactions among different cognitive processes and implementation in OpenCog.  EFTASearch  \
   So I would count these as **separate AI-related projects associated with Epstein's funding**, rather than treating Goertzel as one single correspondence item.
- **AI + consciousness / machine consciousness.**\
   There is a specific document titled around **“Artificial Intelligence and Consciousness.”** It discusses AGI, the risks and benefits of advanced AI, and the question of whether a digital computer could possess human-like cognitive properties.  EFTASearch  \
   This is worth adding to your list because it expands Epstein's AI interests beyond simply “build an intelligent robot.”
- **OpenCog's “super intelligence” objective.**\
   Epstein-related material describes OpenCog's goal not merely as reproducing human intelligence but potentially going **beyond human intelligence**. The documents discuss humanoid robots, virtual agents, natural-language applications and the AtomSpace cognitive architecture.  EFTASearch
- **The Epstein Foundation's own AI biography/material is larger than item 13 suggests.**\
   There are multiple versions of material presenting Epstein as an AI funder. One describes him as having supported OpenCog and Joscha Bach's MicroPsi work; another describes the foundation as funding “multiple artificial intelligence endeavors.”  EFTASearch+1  \
   I'd treat these as **foundation publicity/biographical documents**, though—not independent evidence of Epstein personally writing the claims.
- **The 2017 ASU “Challenges of Artificial Intelligence” workshop.**\
   This is particularly interesting. A February 2017 Origins Project workshop was explicitly devoted to **adverse outcomes from AI**, including AI in financial markets, democracy, military systems, cybersecurity, self-improvement, goal stability, agency and disempowerment.  EFTASearch  \
   Its participant list includes people such as **Dario Amodei, Eric Horvitz, Stuart Russell, Miles Brundage, Nate Soares, Jaan Tallinn, Lawrence Krauss, Ben Zorn and others**.  EFTASearch  \
   **Caveat:** I would _not_ automatically call these people “people Epstein talked to about AI.” The records establish an AI-related event in the Epstein/Origins ecosystem, not necessarily a conversation between Epstein and every participant.
- **PrimeAGI / AGI funding material.**\
   There's also a substantial document for **“Project PrimeAGI,”** whose stated goal was human-level or greater AGI, with a proposed three-year, $6 million initial funding phase.  EFTASearch  \
   Again, this needs attribution analysis before saying Epstein personally authored or funded it.

 ### One correction I'd make to your existing list

 Your **#6, John Brockman/Reality Club**, is weaker as a “person Epstein talked to about AI” entry than the others.

 The actual document shows a forwarded _Reality Club_ discussion of Jaron Lanier's **“The Myth of AI.”** Epstein then responded with a list of people to approach, while Joi Ito replied to Epstein.  Epstein Graph

 So I'd classify it as:

 > **Epstein participated in an AI-related correspondence involving Joi Ito and a forwarded Brockman/Reality Club discussion**

 rather than:

 > “Epstein talked to John Brockman about AI.”

 That's a subtle but important distinction.

 ### There is also a potentially important distinction in your methodology

 I think your document would be considerably stronger if you divided the material into four levels:

 | Category | What belongs there |
| --- | --- |
| **A — Epstein personally discusses AI** | Schank, Nikolic, Ito, MIT robotics/AI, etc. |
| **B — Someone discusses AI directly with Epstein** | Goertzel, Pierce, Summers, Abundis, Ito |
| **C — Epstein's foundation/funding is connected to AI** | OpenCog, MicroPsi, CogPrime, PrimeAGI |
| **D — AI material appears in Epstein's broader correspondence/network** | ASU AI workshop, Reality Club, forwarded papers, AI conferences |

That prevents the number from becoming misleading.

 ### And there's a much bigger finding

 The most significant thing I found is that **“AI” appears to have been a recurring scientific interest for Epstein over many years, not a handful of isolated references.**

 The documentary trail goes from at least the **2001 OpenCog/Goertzel funding claims**, through the **2009–2013 Goertzel/OpenCog proposals**, the **2010 intelligent-machine solicitation**, **2013 AGI/robot discussions**, **2014 AI debate**, **2015–16 robotics/AI material**, and into **2017 AGI/AI-risk material**. The foundation's own material explicitly presents Epstein as a long-term AI funder.  EFTASearch+1

 So I would **not** describe Epstein's AI activity as “5–6 mentions.” It's much larger than that.

 What we **cannot responsibly say yet** is something like “Epstein mentioned AI 143 times,” because a raw search count would mix Epstein's own words with forwarded articles, proposals sent to him, attachments, duplicated email threads, OCR duplicates, and documents merely naming his foundation.

 **If you want, I can take your list and do the next step: build a chronological “Epstein \+ AI” master list, document by document, including every identifiable person, date, EFTA number, what was actually said, and a confidence rating for whether Epstein personally participated.** That would give you a much more defensible lower-bound count.

 One important correction to my earlier answer: I was too conservative when I said I could establish only \~5–6 Epstein-authored AI instances. The newly identified **August 2009 correspondence alone** is an unmistakable additional Epstein-authored AI item, and there are several more.

 The DOJ says the January 30, 2026 production brought the total released material to nearly **3.5 million pages**, and DOJ itself warns that portions of the archive are not electronically searchable or can produce unreliable search results.  Department of Justice+1

 Below is my current **mega-list / research master index**. I am deliberately distinguishing _Epstein personally discussing AI_ from _AI material sent to Epstein_ and from _AI projects associated with his foundation_. That distinction is crucial.

 # Epstein + AI / AGI / Intelligent Machines: Mega List

 ## A. Epstein himself explicitly discussing, proposing, funding, or organizing AI

 ### 1\. Synthetic General Intelligence — John Brockman

 **August 11, 2009 — EFTA00883293 / EFTA01816129**

 This is one of the most important omissions from your original list.

 Epstein told John Brockman that he intended to **fully fund gatherings** around “Synthetic General Intelligence,” specifically describing the area as encompassing:

 - signal processing
- statistical and machine learning
- virtual-world robots
- new programming architecture

 This is unambiguously Epstein's own proposal, not something merely forwarded to him.  EFTASearch+1

 **People:** Jeffrey Epstein, John Brockman, Barnaby Marsh\
 **Classification:** **A — Epstein personally**

---

 ### 2\. Artificial Intelligence and Computational Science research solicitation

 **November 2010 — EFTA00777409**

 Epstein's research solicitation listed **Artificial Intelligence and Computational Science** as a research area.

 It called for work on building an **“intelligent machine”**, distinguished from merely a competent machine, and computational models capable of internal self-revision.

 Al Seckel subsequently worked on/reworked the proposal.

 **People:** Jeffrey Epstein, Al Seckel\
 **Classification:** **A — Epstein's research/funding initiative**

 This is much stronger evidence than simply finding the words “AI” somewhere in an email.

 # The most important quantitative finding

 The search engine currently returns:

 - **62 documents** containing the exact phrase **“Artificial General Intelligence”** across Datasets 9–11.  EFTASearch

- Dataset 9 alone accounts for **44 of the 62 exact “Artificial General Intelligence” hits**.  EFTASearch

 But **62 ≠ 62 conversations with Epstein**, and **250 ≠ 250 AI conversations**. Many are duplicated proposals, attachments, mailing-list material, references, or documents where Epstein isn't the author/recipient.

 That's why I would not yet give you a bogus “Epstein mentioned AI 62 times” number.

[Oct 05, 2026, 4:14 PM EDT] RICKY:

there's this too:

Yes. A few things **really do stick out**, but I’d separate “suspicious” from “criminal” very carefully. The AI records do **not**, by themselves, establish that Epstein was using AI for sexual abuse, blackmail, intelligence operations, or anything like that. What they _do_ show is a surprisingly sustained interest in AGI, robotics, intelligence, deception, and the funding/network around those fields.

 Here are the things I think deserve the most scrutiny.

 ## 1\. Epstein was interested in AGI unusually early — and at a fairly strategic level

 In August 2009, Epstein personally wrote that he intended to **“fully fund” gatherings around Synthetic General Intelligence**, specifically encompassing statistical/machine learning, signal processing, virtual-world robots and new programming architectures. He also wanted “cross pollination” among participants.  Yirah.fi+1

 That's significant because this wasn't:

 > “AI seems interesting.”

 It was essentially:

 > **I want to assemble and fund a network around emerging machine intelligence.**

 And the same 2009 proposal put **Synthetic General Intelligence** alongside subjects such as evolutionary computation, cryptography, genetics, financial systems, power, reputation, trust and **deception**.  Yirah.fi

 ### Why I'd investigate this

 The interesting question isn't “why was Epstein interested in AI?”

 It's:

 **Why was a financier with no obvious conventional AI research career personally organizing interdisciplinary networks around machine intelligence, computational biology, power, deception and financial systems in 2009?**

 That's a legitimate research question.

 It doesn't prove nefarious intent.

---

 # 2\. The “deception” material is probably the single strangest AI correspondence

 This is the one I'd put at the top of the pile.

 In December 2013, Goertzel wrote Epstein about the OpenCog project:

 > Epstein specifically wanted to see a **“deception character.”**

 Goertzel replied that he wanted to help his team create it and said he hoped to demonstrate AI agents **intentionally tricking one another** in a game environment.  Yirah.fi+1

 This wasn't an abstract philosophical discussion.

 It was tied directly to a funding request.

 Goertzel asked Epstein for **$45,000** to maintain the Hong Kong project, and the correspondence discusses the deception-capable game character as a concrete demonstration.  Yirah.fi

 ### But here's the important caveat

 This is **not evidence that Epstein wanted an AI to deceive people in the real world**.

 In context, this appears to be a research demonstration: teaching an AI agent to exhibit deceptive behavior inside a game/simulation.

 Still, Epstein's specific interest in seeing that capability is noteworthy.

 I'd give this **high evidentiary value, low evidentiary value for criminality**.

---

 # 3\. The money trail is more suspicious than the AI itself

 This is, in my opinion, the strongest genuinely uncomfortable part.

 In December 2013, Goertzel proposed that Epstein provide **$45,000** required as the corporate contribution/matching component for a Hong Kong government research grant.

 Instead of simply sending the money directly, Goertzel proposed routing it through **Humanity+**, a 501(c)(3), which would then redirect the money to Hong Kong Polytechnic University.  Yirah.fi+1

 Then there's a particularly interesting sequence:

 - Goertzel proposes the Humanity+ route.
- Epstein agrees to send the $45,000.
- Epstein explicitly asks about the **deception character**.
- Richard Kahn, working for Epstein, handles the transfer.
- The records subsequently show **$45,000 coming back from Humanity+**, followed by discussion of sending **$60,000 from STC to Goertzel's Novamente LLC**.  Yirah.fi+1

 That's considerably more interesting than “Epstein funded AI.”

 ### Why?

 Because it creates a complicated chain:

 **Epstein → Humanity+ → Hong Kong research**

 followed by

 **Humanity+ → Epstein's organization → $60,000 → Goertzel's company**

 The documents themselves don't establish that anything illegal occurred. There may have been perfectly legitimate grant/accounting reasons.

 But this is exactly the kind of thing I'd want an investigator/accountant to reconstruct **transaction by transaction**.

---

 # 4\. Goertzel explicitly said the Foundation hadn't actually funded iCog — despite allowing Epstein's name to be associated with it

 This is another fascinating one.

 In 2014, Goertzel wrote that:

 > the Epstein Foundation **had not actually funded iCog Labs**.

 He explained that Epstein's money had funded the **OpenCog Hong Kong** operation, while iCog had been seed-funded by another investor and was then self-supporting.  Yirah.fi+1

 But Goertzel also said that, because Epstein had been generous over the years, he was willing to let a press release **associate the Epstein Foundation with iCog Labs**, provided the wording accurately described the situation.  Yirah.fi

 That's an unusually explicit distinction between:

 **what Epstein actually funded**

 and

 **what Epstein's public association with the project could imply.**

 That deserves scrutiny.

 It doesn't prove deception—but it is precisely the sort of discrepancy that should be documented carefully.

---

 # 5\. Epstein wasn't merely donating; he was asking for specific technical demonstrations

 This is another thing I think gets lost when people summarize the material as “Epstein funded Goertzel.”

 In December 2013, Goertzel tells Epstein:

 > “your focus on deception is an interesting one”

 and says one goal is to demonstrate AI agents intentionally tricking each other.  Yirah.fi

 Epstein then asks:

 > **“i want to see the deception character, how do we send the funds?”**  Yirah.fi

 That's a much more direct expression of interest than simply signing a philanthropic grant.

 It suggests Epstein was actually **following particular capabilities being developed**, at least within this project.

---

 # 6\. Epstein wanted to accelerate AGI rather than merely study AI

 Goertzel repeatedly pitched Epstein on progressively larger projects.

 One 2011 proposal asked Epstein to contribute **$1.5 million** toward a $3 million, four-year AGI program described as a **“Sputnik of AGI.”** The intended result was an AGI controlling both a videogame character and a Hanson humanoid robot with roughly child-level intelligence.  Yirah.fi

 And Goertzel explicitly thanked Epstein for already funding the smaller OpenCog AGI project.

 That's important because the relationship wasn't:

 **Epstein → passive philanthropist**

 It was closer to:

 **Epstein → wealthy prospective patron → repeated AGI proposals → technical demonstrations → increasingly ambitious funding requests.**

---

 # 7\. The “child-level intelligence + humanoid robot” objective is striking, but not inherently sinister

 The proposals repeatedly use concepts like:

 - toddler-level intelligence
- humanoid robots
- virtual characters
- machine perception
- natural-language dialogue
- emotional/social intelligence
- autonomous agents
- AGI
- self-improvement

 The 2011 proposal explicitly envisioned an AGI controlling a Hanson Robokind humanoid robot.  Yirah.fi

 That sounds extraordinary today.

 But technically, **this was a recognizable research goal in AGI/robotics**. There's nothing inherently suspicious about wanting a humanoid robot with child-level cognitive abilities.

 The suspicious question is **why Epstein was so interested and what exactly he wanted demonstrated**, not the technology itself.

---

 # 8\. The “power” connection is interesting

 Go back to Epstein's 2009 list.

 Immediately adjacent to his Synthetic General Intelligence gathering was:

 > **“Conference on Power”**

 covering political, financial, intellectual and physical power, including:

 - reputation
- awe
- trust
- **deception**
- reciprocity
- group selection
- behavior.  Yirah.fi

 And elsewhere in the same overall intellectual program he wanted discussions of:

 - financial systems
- computers
- unemployment
- derivatives
- intelligence
- evolutionary dynamics
- genetics
- cryptography.

 Again, I would **not connect these dots into a conspiracy without evidence**.

 But as a research pattern, it is interesting.

 Epstein's intellectual agenda appears to have been unusually focused on **systems of intelligence, influence, power and complex adaptive behavior**.

---

 # 9\. The AI funding continued after Epstein's 2008 conviction

 This is probably the most morally damning aspect of the story, although it's about Epstein/Goertzel rather than the technology itself.

 Goertzel continued accepting funding after Epstein's 2008 conviction.

 The newly released correspondence shows Goertzel seeking additional funding in the following years. Business Insider reports that the emails show roughly **$200,000+ in funding between 2001 and 2018**, while Goertzel himself now gives a higher approximate lifetime figure of **$360,000** depending on what is counted.  Business Insider+1

 The South China Morning Post independently reconstructed at least **$113,000 from 2010–2015** connected to OpenCog and reported that the money helped Goertzel qualify for approximately **HK$8.9 million in Hong Kong government grants**.  South China Morning Post

 And this gets much worse reputationally because Goertzel later acknowledged that he had failed to properly investigate Epstein.

---

 # 10\. Goertzel's 2015 reaction to Epstein's sex-abuse allegations is extremely uncomfortable

 This is probably the **most damaging non-AI-related fact inside the AI correspondence**.

 According to the South China Morning Post's reconstruction, when Epstein's sexual-abuse allegations resurfaced in 2015, Goertzel was still seeking another **$25,000** and characterized the allegations in an email in extraordinarily minimizing terms.  South China Morning Post

 Goertzel now says he regrets his relationship with Epstein and acknowledges that he failed to do adequate due diligence.  Ben Goertzel

 So if you're asking:

 > **“What's the worst thing I found involving Epstein's AI network?”**

 I'd rank **continued financial solicitation/acceptance after Epstein's conviction and the way his allegations were characterized** above anything about the AI technology itself.

---

 # 11\. There is an interesting “network-building” angle

 The 2009 email is particularly revealing because Epstein wasn't simply proposing a research grant.

 He described gatherings involving:

 > meetings, hikes, coffee, “cross pollination”

 and wanted to **fully fund them**.  Yirah.fi

 That sounds more like **network formation** than conventional academic philanthropy.

 And the proposed participants/subjects intersected with:

 - AI
- evolutionary biology
- genetics
- cryptography
- finance
- power
- neuroscience
- computation.

 That fits a broader pattern in Epstein's activities: bringing together elite scientists, academics, financiers and technologists.

 Again, **network-building isn't evidence of wrongdoing**. But if you're investigating motive, this is much more interesting than simply counting the word “AI.”

---

 # What I DON'T see

 This is equally important.

 From the material I've examined so far, I **haven't found credible evidence that:**

 - Epstein was secretly developing an AI system for sexual exploitation.
- Epstein used AI to generate or distribute child sexual abuse material.
- Epstein had a secret AGI that actually achieved human-level intelligence.
- Epstein used AI for blackmail.
- Epstein was running an intelligence-agency AI operation.
- OpenCog was secretly an Epstein surveillance system.
- Epstein directed Goertzel to build an AI for criminal purposes.
- Epstein possessed some hidden “Epstein AI” capable of autonomous operation.

 Those would be extraordinary claims, and **the documents currently in evidence don't justify them**.

 # My “suspicion ranking”

 If I were investigating this seriously, I'd prioritize:

 ### 🔴 Very worth investigating

 **1\. The $45K → Humanity+ → Hong Kong PolyU → $60K/Novamente sequence.**\
 The accounting and purpose need to be reconstructed from bank records and all related emails.  Yirah.fi+1

 **2\. Epstein's specific interest in the “deception character.”**\
 Especially why _that_ capability caught his attention and what conversations surrounded it.  Yirah.fi

 **3\. The discrepancy between actual Epstein funding and public association with iCog.**\
 Goertzel himself explicitly distinguished the two.  Yirah.fi

 **4\. Epstein's 2009 interdisciplinary network-building proposal.**\
 The combination of AGI + power + deception + finance + genetics + cryptography is unusually broad.  Yirah.fi

 ### 🟠 Interesting but not inherently suspicious

 **5\. Epstein's interest in humanoid robots.**

 **6\. Epstein's desire to attend AGI conferences.**

 **7\. His relationship with Minsky, Ito, Bach and Goertzel.**

 **8\. His interest in machine consciousness.**

 **9\. His interest in AI agents capable of deception in simulated environments.**

 All of these are plausible interests for an unusually wealthy science patron.

 ### 🟢 Basically not suspicious by themselves

 - AI-written haiku
- machine learning
- robotics demonstrations
- natural-language processing
- toddler-level AI
- virtual-world agents
- AI conferences
- OpenCog development
- AI safety research.

 ## The thing I'd investigate next

 There is a **much more interesting question than “did Epstein like AI?”**

 It's:

 > **What did Epstein actually want from the AI researchers, and what did he ask them to build or demonstrate that wasn't part of their normal research agenda?**

 The **“deception character”** is the first place I'd dig.

 I'd want to pull the _entire Goertzel ↔ Epstein thread_ surrounding December 2013—not just the two emails we've found—and reconstruct:

 **Epstein's question → Goertzel's proposal → technical objective → funding request → money movement → resulting demonstration.**

 Then I'd do the same thing for the **2009 Synthetic General Intelligence gatherings** and the **2011 $3M “Sputnik of AGI” proposal**.

 That would tell us whether we're looking at **a wealthy eccentric funding cutting-edge AI**, or something more unusual. At the moment, the former is clearly supported; the latter is **possible as a question worth investigating, but not established by the evidence I've seen**.

[Oct 05, 2026, 4:17 PM EDT] RICKY:

there's this too:

Yes. I think there is a coherent pattern worth examining—but I would frame it as **political economy and concentration of power**, not as evidence of a hidden Epstein/AI master plan.

 The thing that strikes me most is this:

 > **AI is becoming extraordinarily good at extracting value from people, organizations, data, attention, energy and intellectual work faster than institutions have figured out how to distribute the resulting gains.**

 And that pattern is visible independently of Epstein.

 ## 1\. The weirdest part: AI can be highly productive without producing broad prosperity

 This is actually showing up in current economic research.

 The ILO reported in May 2026 that AI can produce **10–70% productivity gains at the individual/task level**, but those gains have not yet translated into clear aggregate productivity growth. Gains are concentrated in large, digitally advanced companies, while many firms see little effect beyond pilots.  International Labour Organization

 That's a fascinating disconnect:

 **Worker gets faster → company gets more output per worker → shareholder/capital owner captures much of the gain → economy as a whole doesn't necessarily become visibly richer.**

 And the IMF's 2026 analysis estimates the labor-time value currently saved by AI at approximately **$2.7 trillion annually**, while finding that AI usage is highly concentrated in relatively small professional enclaves in developing economies.  International Monetary Fund

 So your intuition isn't crazy.

 The question isn't necessarily:

 > “Does AI create value?”

 It clearly does.

 It's:

 > **Who owns the machinery that captures the value?**

---

 # 2\. The extraction is happening at several layers simultaneously

 Think of AI as sitting on top of an enormous extraction stack:

 ### Human attention

 Your conversations, searches, clicks, images, writing, preferences and behavior become inputs into increasingly sophisticated systems.

 ### Human knowledge

 Models ingest enormous quantities of accumulated human intellectual production.

 ### Human labor

 AI increasingly substitutes for or augments:

 - programmers
- writers
- designers
- analysts
- researchers
- customer service
- translators
- administrative workers
- consultants
- lawyers
- educators.

 ### Corporate data

 Companies are turning their internal documents, communications and workflows into proprietary AI systems.

 ### Physical resources

 AI requires:

 - chips
- electricity
- water
- land
- buildings
- transmission infrastructure
- cooling systems
- minerals.

 ### Capital

 And ultimately, the people controlling the compute infrastructure capture a disproportionate share of the economic upside.

 That's the part I think is genuinely important.

---

 # 3\. The physical extraction is getting enormous

 This isn't theoretical anymore.

 The IEA says global data-center electricity consumption rose **17% in 2025**, while electricity consumption by AI-focused data centers increased approximately **50%**. It projects total data-center electricity consumption to roughly double from **485 TWh in 2025 to 950 TWh in 2030**, with AI-focused facilities tripling.  IEA+1

 And water is part of the equation.

 The IEA estimated that a 100-MW hyperscale U.S. data center can consume around **2 million liters of water per day** when direct and indirect water use are included. Global data-center water consumption was estimated at about **560 billion liters annually**, potentially rising to around **1.2 trillion liters by 2030**.  IEA Blob Storage

 So there's a very literal extraction loop:

 **human activity → data → AI → capital → compute → electricity/water/land → more AI → more capital**

 The benefits are potentially enormous.

 But so are the externalities.

---

 # 4\. And there's an important asymmetry

 This is the thing I'd watch most closely.

 Imagine a company replaces 10,000 hours of human labor with AI.

 There are two possible futures.

 ### Future A — broadly distributed

 The company saves money.

 Workers work fewer hours.

 Prices fall.

 People receive some combination of:

 - higher wages
- shorter workweeks
- better public services
- dividends
- universal benefits.

 **Society captures the productivity gain.**

 ### Future B — concentrated

 The company saves money.

 Workers are laid off.

 Remaining employees are expected to produce more.

 Prices don't fall much.

 Executive compensation rises.

 Shareholders capture the savings.

 The AI company receives enormous recurring revenue.

 **Capital captures the productivity gain.**

 The technology is identical.

 The political economy is completely different.

 And the evidence so far suggests we're seeing a mixture, with substantial risks of the second outcome. The IMF finds that AI adoption can actually **increase wealth inequality**, because high-income workers are better positioned to complement AI while capital owners capture higher returns.  International Monetary Fund

---

 # 5\. Here's where Epstein becomes interesting—but not necessarily conspiratorial

 I think we should separate two stories.

 ### Story 1: “Epstein secretly planned today's AI economy.”

 **I don't see evidence for that.**

 That would be an enormous leap.

 ### Story 2: “Epstein was interested in a class of technologies concerned with intelligence, prediction, influence, cognition, deception and systems of power—and wealthy networks were already thinking about these things long before today's AI boom.”

 **There is considerably more evidence for this.**

 And that's interesting.

 Because look at the conceptual overlap:

 **Epstein-era interests:**

 - intelligence
- artificial intelligence
- machine learning
- genetics
- neuroscience
- financial systems
- prediction
- reputation
- trust
- deception
- social behavior
- power
- elite scientific networks.

 Then look at today's AI economy:

 - behavioral prediction
- personalized persuasion
- automated decision-making
- financial optimization
- surveillance
- labor substitution
- information control
- algorithmic reputation
- concentration of compute
- automated persuasion.

 You don't need Epstein to explain this.

 These are natural consequences of the economics of computational intelligence.

 But the overlap is worth studying.

---

 # 6\. The really uncomfortable resource isn't electricity

 It's **control over intelligence**.

 That may ultimately be more important than energy.

 Historically, we've had:

 **industrial capital → control of physical production**

 Then:

 **financial capital → control of capital allocation**

 Now potentially:

 **computational capital → control of cognitive production**

 If a small number of organizations control the strongest models, chips, cloud infrastructure and data, they increasingly control the infrastructure through which everyone else performs cognitive work.

 The IMF explicitly identifies **concentration of frontier models and compute** as a structural source of a growing resilience gap between AI leaders and laggards.  International Monetary Fund

 That is potentially a much bigger story than “AI takes jobs.”

---

 # 7\. And there's a feedback loop that could become extremely powerful

 Consider:

 **More users**

 ↓

 more data / feedback

 ↓

 better models

 ↓

 more users

 ↓

 more revenue

 ↓

 more GPUs

 ↓

 better models

 ↓

 more automation

 ↓

 more revenue

 ↓

 more compute

 ↓

 more automation

 That is a **positive feedback loop**.

 And it favors incumbents.

 That's why the current AI race is becoming extraordinarily capital-intensive.

 A remarkable example: Anthropic's 2026 IPO filing reportedly disclosed approximately **$518 billion of long-term AI infrastructure commitments**, much of it non-cancelable, involving Google, Amazon, Microsoft, Broadcom and others. Its 2025 revenue reached $4.6 billion while it still reported more than $8 billion in losses.  Reuters+1

 That's not a normal software business.

 It's an infrastructure race.

---

 # 8\. And that creates a strange situation

 AI companies are effectively saying:

 > “We need hundreds of billions of dollars because AI will transform everything.”

 Investors say:

 > “Yes.”

 Governments say:

 > “We need to support AI because we can't fall behind.”

 Utilities say:

 > “We need to build massive amounts of electricity infrastructure.”

 Communities say:

 > “Why is our power/water/land being consumed?”

 Workers say:

 > “Why am I being replaced or monitored?”

 And meanwhile:

 **the companies building the infrastructure become increasingly indispensable.**

 That's where I think the real political question lies.

---

 # 9\. There's another thing that looks off: the public-benefit argument is remarkably vague

 We're constantly told AI will eventually produce:

 - scientific breakthroughs
- cures
- abundance
- education
- productivity
- personalized medicine
- better government
- cheaper goods
- higher living standards.

 Those things **could happen**.

 But “AI will benefit humanity” isn't an economic distribution mechanism.

 Someone has to decide:

 **Who owns the AI?**

 **Who owns the compute?**

 **Who receives the productivity gains?**

 **Who pays for the electricity?**

 **Who owns the data?**

 **Who bears unemployment risk?**

 **Who gets displaced?**

 **Who receives compensation for training data?**

 **Who controls the models?**

 **Who gets access to the best models?**

 Those are political questions, not technological ones.

---

 # 10\. The Epstein material actually makes me more interested in this distinction

 Because the Epstein documents don't primarily show:

 > “AI is evil.”

 They show something subtler:

 **Very wealthy people were interested in machine intelligence as an instrument of scientific and social capability long before ordinary people had meaningful access to it.**

 That's not surprising.

 But it raises a historically important question:

 > **When a technology capable of amplifying cognition becomes extraordinarily expensive, who gets to control it first?**

 That's potentially the real story.

 And it is happening now.

---

 # 11\. There's an even darker possibility—but we need evidence

 There are three possible trajectories.

 ### ① AI as a public utility

 Compute becomes abundant.

 Models become cheap.

 Workers capture productivity gains.

 Open models proliferate.

 Education, medicine and scientific research become dramatically cheaper.

 **This is the optimistic scenario.**

 ### ② AI as a new industrial oligopoly

 A few companies control:

 - models
- chips
- cloud
- data
- energy
- distribution.

 Everyone else rents intelligence from them.

 **This is the scenario I'd currently worry about most.**

 ### ③ AI as an infrastructure of behavioral control

 AI becomes deeply integrated into:

 - employment
- finance
- insurance
- policing
- education
- advertising
- healthcare
- government
- information systems.

 Then the important resource isn't merely intelligence.

 It's **the ability to predict and influence people at scale.**

 That would be a fundamentally different kind of power.

---

 # And here's the part I'd really investigate

 If we're going to continue down the Epstein rabbit hole, I wouldn't primarily search for:

 > **“Did Epstein secretly build AI?”**

 I'd search for:

 > **“Who was Epstein trying to connect to whom around AI, neuroscience, genetics, finance, prediction, behavioral science and computational systems?”**

 Because the potentially important thing isn't an AI machine sitting somewhere.

 It's the **network**.

 I'd map:

 **Epstein → Goertzel → OpenCog → Minsky → MIT Media Lab → Joi Ito → Joscha Bach → Hanson Robotics → Humanity+ → AI conferences → finance/technology people**

 and then separately map:

 **Epstein → genetics → neuroscience → behavioral science → prediction → finance → power/deception**

 Then look for intersections.

 If there are **people, organizations, money flows or projects sitting at the intersections of those two networks**, _that_ would be genuinely interesting.

 And I'd apply a very high evidentiary standard: distinguish **documented fact → strong inference → speculation**. Otherwise it's incredibly easy to turn a fascinating network into a conspiracy theory that the documents don't actually support.

 The broader economic concern, meanwhile, doesn't require Epstein at all: current IMF, ILO and IEA evidence already shows **concentrated AI gains, substantial infrastructure/resource demands, and significant risks that productivity gains accrue disproportionately to capital and already-advantaged workers.**  International Monetary Fund+2

[Oct 05, 2026, 4:21 PM EDT] RICKY:

seems like these Blank Ai get overwhelmed with the mess and repeat stuff at the end, but it's interesting that i didn't share my work with it, it doesn't knwo anything about me.

I think it is **genuinely concerning**, but not because the evidence points to a secret AI plot. The more defensible concern is arguably more serious: **we may be building a technology that massively increases the ability to extract value while our institutions are still organized around an older economy.**

 And the data increasingly support your observation that the benefits and burdens aren't being distributed symmetrically.

 ## The paradox

 There is a striking contradiction in the current AI story:

 **AI is getting better at making individual humans and companies more productive, while the economy isn't yet showing commensurate broad-based productivity gains.**

 The ILO's May 2026 review found task-level productivity improvements commonly in the **10–70% range**, but those gains have not yet translated into clear aggregate productivity growth. Gains are concentrated in larger, digitally advanced companies.  International Labour Organization

 The IMF's July 2026 analysis estimates the labor-time value currently saved by AI at about **$2.7 trillion per year**—roughly 3.4% of the GDP of its sample countries—but finds that the gains disproportionately accrue to higher-paid occupations and remain extremely concentrated in many poorer countries.  IMF eLibrary

 So your question becomes very important:

 > **If AI is generating trillions of dollars worth of potential productivity, where is that value going?**

 That's the issue I'd focus on.

---

 # 1\. AI may be creating an enormous extraction machine

 Consider what gets fed into the system:

 **People**

 → their labor\
 → their knowledge\
 → their conversations\
 → their creative work\
 → their behavioral data\
 → their attention

 **Businesses**

 → their documents\
 → proprietary knowledge\
 → workflows\
 → customer interactions\
 → intellectual property

 **The planet**

 → electricity\
 → water\
 → minerals\
 → land\
 → manufacturing capacity

 And what comes out?

 **Computational capital.**

 The really strange thing is that the inputs are widely distributed while ownership of the resulting infrastructure is highly concentrated.

 That's a classic recipe for inequality.

 The IMF explicitly warns that AI diffusion is creating dependencies on a relatively small number of frontier models and concentrated compute.  International Monetary Fund

---

 # 2\. And the infrastructure is becoming enormous

 This isn't just software anymore.

 The IEA says data-center electricity consumption increased **17% in 2025**, while electricity consumption by AI-focused data centers increased even faster. Its current base case has global data-center electricity consumption approaching **945 TWh by 2030**—roughly double 2024 levels.  IEA+1

 That's an extraordinary transfer of physical resources toward computation.

 And there's an important geographical asymmetry.

 A community can have:

 - the water,
- the electricity infrastructure,
- the land,
- the transmission lines,
- the tax incentives,

 while another company, perhaps thousands of miles away, owns the AI system consuming them.

 The United Nations University explicitly describes AI's environmental footprint as a **governance and justice problem**, because benefits can cross borders while water, land, energy and pollution burdens remain concentrated in particular communities.  United Nations University

 That's a classic externalization problem.

---

 # 3\. And some of the costs are literally being pushed onto ordinary people

 There's new U.S. evidence that's particularly interesting.

 A 2026 NBER study examining data centers found:

  - employment and establishments increase locally;
- construction increases;
- **house prices rise**;
- **public water withdrawals rise**;
- and eventually **electricity prices increase**.

 So data centers can create economic activity while simultaneously putting pressure on housing, electricity and water.  National Bureau of Economic Research

 That's a very different thing from:

 > “AI makes everyone richer.”

 It's closer to:

 > **AI creates a new economic center, while some of the costs are socialized locally.**

 That doesn't automatically make the development bad. But it means we need to ask **who captures the upside and who absorbs the downside.**

---

 # 4\. This is where I think your “AI should have prevented this” point is really interesting

 You're essentially asking:

 > **If we're building systems capable of reasoning, forecasting, optimization and analyzing enormous quantities of information, why aren't they being used to solve the problems that are getting worse?**

 That's a legitimate challenge.

 But there's a hidden assumption:

 ### Intelligence ≠ agency.

 AI can tell you:

 > “This policy will increase inequality.”

 It cannot necessarily decide:

 > “Therefore we will redistribute the gains.”

 AI can tell you:

 > “This data center will increase local water stress.”

 It cannot necessarily say:

 > “Therefore the corporation will pay the full social cost.”

 AI can tell you:

 > “Automating these workers will increase shareholder returns while reducing their bargaining power.”

 It cannot decide:

 > “Therefore the workers should receive 30% of the productivity gains.”

 Those are **political decisions**.

 And that's the uncomfortable thing:

 ### We may be increasing intelligence faster than we're increasing governance.

---

 # 5\. There's a deeper structural problem

 Suppose AI increases productivity by 30%.

 That's potentially wonderful.

 But there are at least four possible outcomes:

 **A.** Workers keep their jobs and earn more.

 **B.** Workers work fewer hours for the same income.

 **C.** Prices fall and consumers capture the benefit.

 **D.** The company reduces labor costs and shareholders capture the benefit.

 AI doesn't determine which happens.

 **Ownership and bargaining power do.**

 And the ILO is already warning that the principal risks emerging from generative AI include inequality, weaker employment opportunities for young people, reduced worker autonomy and deteriorating job quality.  International Labour Organization

 That is why I think the central AI question isn't:

 > _“How intelligent will AI become?”_

 It's:

 > **“Who gets the productivity dividend?”**

---

 # 6\. There's another layer: AI is unusually good at optimizing extraction

 This is something I think deserves much more attention.

 The same technology that can optimize:

 - drug discovery
- energy systems
- logistics
- scientific research

 can also optimize:

 - advertising
- pricing
- credit scoring
- worker monitoring
- customer retention
- political persuasion
- financial trading
- targeted sales
- automated surveillance.

 And there's a fundamental economic asymmetry here.

 A corporation doesn't need AI to solve poverty to make enormous money from AI.

 It needs AI to make **one of its existing activities more profitable.**

 So if the economic incentives are:

 > maximize revenue / minimize labor / maximize engagement / maximize prediction,

 AI will become extraordinarily good at those objectives.

 That's not an AI conspiracy.

 It's **optimization following the objective function supplied by the institution using it.**

---

 # 7\. That's where Epstein becomes relevant—but differently than we were discussing

 After looking at the AI material, I actually think the most interesting Epstein question isn't:

 > “Did Epstein know about AI?”

 Obviously he did.

 Nor:

 > “Was Epstein secretly controlling modern AI?”

 There's no evidence for that.

 The more interesting historical question is:

 > **Why were wealthy networks interested in intelligence, prediction, behavioral science, genetics, financial systems, deception and computational systems at the same time?**

 Because those things share a common theme:

 ### **They increase the ability to understand, predict and influence complex systems.**

 That's an extremely valuable form of power.

 And AI is essentially the industrialization of that capability.

---

 # 8\. The “deception” issue therefore looks more interesting in this broader context

 Remember Epstein's specific interest in Goertzel's **“deception character.”**

 I don't think that proves anything sinister.

 But put it next to modern AI research and something becomes conceptually important:

 AI isn't merely learning to **answer questions**.

 It's increasingly capable of:

 - modeling other agents;
- anticipating their behavior;
- negotiating;
- persuading;
- strategically withholding information;
- optimizing against objectives;
- adapting to feedback.

 Those capabilities can be extraordinarily beneficial.

 They can also become extremely powerful tools of **asymmetric influence**.

 The important distinction is that Epstein's documents don't demonstrate he was secretly pursuing that second application.

 But the broader technological trajectory makes the question worth taking seriously.

---

 # 9\. The thing that concerns me most is concentration

 This is where I'd put the risk level.

 Not:

 **“AI will become evil.”**

 More like:

 **“AI will become indispensable while ownership remains concentrated.”**

 Imagine that five or ten organizations eventually control most of:

 - frontier models
- advanced chips
- cloud compute
- data
- AI agents
- robotics platforms
- scientific AI
- enterprise AI.

 Then virtually every other industry becomes dependent on them.

 That's a profound shift in economic power.

 The IMF explicitly describes concentrated frontier models and compute as creating a **resilience gap** between AI leaders and lagging economies.  International Monetary Fund

---

 # 10\. And there's a particularly troubling feedback loop

 This is the scenario I'd watch.

 **Capital**

 ↓

 buys compute

 ↓

 compute improves AI

 ↓

 AI replaces/augments labor

 ↓

 productivity increases

 ↓

 profits increase

 ↓

 more capital

 ↓

 more compute

 ↓

 better AI

 ↓

 more automation

 ↓

 more profits

 That's a **capital-compute feedback loop**.

 If ownership isn't broadly distributed, the process can become self-reinforcing.

 The people who own the productive infrastructure accumulate more resources to build the next generation of productive infrastructure.

 Everyone else rents access.

 That's not science fiction.

 It's a familiar pattern from previous industrial revolutions—except the productive asset is increasingly **cognitive capacity**.

---

 # 11\. But I don't think we're doomed

 This is important.

 The same evidence that worries me also shows that **the outcome isn't predetermined**.

 The ILO explicitly identifies:

 - competition policy
- collective bargaining
- skills investment
- social protection
- worker participation
- data protection
- institutional adaptation

 as mechanisms that can determine whether AI productivity gains are broadly distributed.  International Labour Organization

 In other words:

 **Technology creates possibilities. Institutions determine who gets them.**

 That distinction is enormous.

 The distribution problem is real, and the concentration of compute/capital is concerning.

 I don't see evidence that Epstein explains the modern AI economy.

 ### The broader historical pattern: **8/10**

 **A technology capable of massively amplifying intelligence is arriving at the same time that wealth, data, compute and infrastructure are becoming increasingly concentrated.**

 That's the genuinely consequential thing.

 **Epstein → Goertzel → OpenCog → SingularityNET / Hyperon**

 # 1\. The most important document I found may be the 2014 “virtual animal” proposal

 He was funding a **particular cognitive architecture and a group of people attempting to construct general intelligence.**

 That's considerably more substantive.

 # 20\. The most important thing I would _not_ do

 I would **not** make the investigation about finding a secret cabal.

 That framing will actually make it harder to discover what's happening.

 Instead, I'd investigate **capital and institutional continuity**.

 The questions are:

 ### Who funded whom?

 ### Who sat on whose boards?

 ### Which nonprofits became companies?

 ### Which researchers moved together?

 ### Which intellectual projects became commercial products?

 ### Which foundations funded the next generation?

 ### Which companies acquired the technology?

 ### Which investors financed the infrastructure?

 ### Which government grants followed private funding?

 ### Which datasets became valuable?

 ### Who owns those datasets now?

 ### Who owns the compute?

 ### Who owns the resulting models?

 That's where the real power is.

 # The three investigations I'd do next

 If we're going to go **really deep**, I would now split this into three parallel investigations.

 ## Investigation A — The Epstein → AGI money trail

 I'd reconstruct every identifiable payment from approximately 2001–2019:

 **Epstein / foundations**

 → Goertzel\
 → OpenCog\
 → Humanity+\
 → Hong Kong Polytechnic\
 → Hanson Robotics\
 → Bach\
 → AGI conferences\
 → MIT-related projects\
 → other AI researchers.

 Then identify **every intermediary account/entity and recipient**.

 This is the cleanest way to establish whether the network was merely philanthropic or whether there was unusual financial structuring.

 ## Investigation B — The people who survived Epstein

 Build a personnel genealogy:

 **Epstein-era person**

 → 2019 position

 → 2022 position

 → 2026 position

 and map:

 - boards;
- companies;
- nonprofits;
- investors;
- universities;
- government grants;
- conferences;
- publications.

 That will tell us whether there is **actual personnel continuity**.

 This is the part I think could become much bigger than Epstein.

 Map:

 → venture capital

 → chips

 → data centers

 → government subsidies.

 Because that gets directly to the concern you raised earlier:

 > **Is AI primarily becoming a technology for human flourishing—or a new mechanism for concentrating control over intelligence, data, capital and infrastructure?**

 That question can be investigated empirically.

 And frankly, **I think that is now a more important investigation than trying to prove that Epstein secretly controlled AI.**

 The evidence I've found so far supports something subtler and potentially more consequential:

 > **Epstein was an early wealthy patron of an emerging AGI/transhumanist network. Some of the people and institutions he funded survived him. Their work evolved into today's decentralized AGI, biomedical-AI and autonomous-agent ecosystems. Separately, the financial system is now pouring enormous amounts of capital into AI compute as an investable infrastructure class. There are intriguing intersections between those histories, but I have not found evidence establishing that they constitute one continuing coordinated operation.**

 That's where I'd draw the line **for now**.

 And there is enough here to justify going substantially deeper—particularly into **the complete Humanity\+ financial records, the Goertzel/OpenCog corporate genealogy, the Black Foundation proposal and its actual disposition, and the post-2019 movement of the people involved.**

 # 2\. The fundamental thing I see: AI is becoming a new layer of capital

 This is perhaps the most important observation.

 Historically, societies have had productive assets:

 **Land → factories → machines → computers → networks → data → AI.**

 AI adds something qualitatively unusual:

 ### It can perform cognitive labor.

 Not perfectly, not autonomously in every context—but increasingly.

 So whoever controls the AI infrastructure potentially controls a growing portion of society's **productive intelligence**.

 And that means the question:

 > “Who owns AI?”

 isn't really about software.

 It's increasingly equivalent to asking:

 > **“Who owns a significant portion of the machinery through which society thinks and works?”**

 The OECD is explicitly warning about this. Its July 2026 analysis says AI has significant competition risks arising from concentration of **compute, data and skills**, vertical integration, first-mover advantages, preferential partnerships and control over infrastructure. It notes that the three largest cloud providers accounted for about **74% of global cloud market share in 2023**, while NVIDIA accounted for roughly **90% of the GPU market** at that time.  OECD

 That's not a conspiracy.

 It's an economic structure.

 Because it means AI isn't merely:

 And Apollo/Blackstone already led a **$35 billion initial platform** intended to support more than 20 GW of AI compute capacity, including capacity for frontier labs such as Anthropic and OpenAI.  Apollo Global Management, Inc.+1

 That changes the picture.

 These are natural directions of technological development.

 But there **is a conceptual continuity**:

 > **understand intelligence → model behavior → predict behavior → automate decisions → allow machines to act → allow machines to transact.**

 And we're approaching the final steps now.

---

 # 6\. The next transformation is much more important than chatbots

 This is something I think most public discussion still understates.

 We're moving from:

 ### AI as information retrieval

 to

 ### AI as labor

 to

 ### AI as agent

 to

 ### AI as economic actor.

 MIT is explicitly discussing an emerging “agent economy” in which potentially billions or trillions of AI agents could interact.

 The infrastructure being developed includes:

 These are natural directions of technological development.

  - identity;
- discovery;
- verification;
- reputation;
- trust;
- payments;
- legal services;
- insurance;
- coordination.  MIT Sloan

 The MIT Media Lab's current agentic-AI work similarly identifies **identity, trust, reputation, payments, evaluation, coordination and governance** as missing infrastructure for this new economy.  MIT Media Lab

 Think about what that means.

 The Internet gave people a way to communicate.

 AI agents could give machines a way to:

 **represent people, negotiate for people, buy things, sell things, make decisions, form contracts and interact with other machines.**

 That is an entirely new economic layer.

---

 # 7\. And here is where ordinary citizens could lose something very important

 Imagine your AI agent becomes your:

 - financial negotiator;
- insurance intermediary;
- shopping agent;
- employment agent;
- healthcare assistant;
- government interface;
- personal researcher;
- legal assistant.

 That sounds fantastic.

 But who owns the infrastructure through which your agent operates?

 If the answer is:

 **Google / Microsoft / Amazon / OpenAI / Meta / Apple / Nvidia / banks / telecoms / financial platforms**

 then your “personal AI” may actually be a **tenant inside someone else's economic ecosystem**.

 The agent may represent you.

 But the infrastructure provider controls:

 - the model;
- the interface;
- the identity layer;
- the data;
- the transaction rails;
- the reputation system;
- the APIs;
- potentially the payments.

 That's where the possibility of **dependency** becomes much more serious.

---

 # 8\. The OECD is basically describing this risk

 Its 2026 analysis warns about:

 **data feedback loops → network effects → user lock-in → vertical integration → exclusive partnerships → gatekeeping.**  OECD

 That's the mechanism.

 It doesn't require anyone to sit in a room and conspire.

 Suppose Company A has:

 - 500 million users;
- enormous data;
- the best cloud infrastructure;
- the best model;
- the most developers;
- the biggest payment system.

 Every new user makes the ecosystem stronger.

 Every new user produces more data.

 More data improves the product.

 Better product attracts more users.

 More users attract developers.

 Developers create more applications.

 More applications make leaving harder.

 That's a **self-reinforcing concentration loop**.

---

 # 9\. And AI could make that loop more powerful than previous technologies

 Because AI can participate in the loop itself.

 Traditional software:

 > user → software → result.

 AI:

 > user → AI → observes user → adapts → predicts user → influences user → gets feedback → improves.

 The system can potentially learn:

 - what you want;
- what you're likely to buy;
- what arguments persuade you;
- what makes you anxious;
- what makes you angry;
- which information you trust;
- how much you will pay;
- which alternative you are likely to choose.

 That makes the **behavioral data layer** extraordinarily valuable.

 And now imagine millions of agents doing this continuously.

 The economic value of prediction becomes enormous.

---

 # 10\. This is where the Epstein-era “reputation / trust / deception” interest becomes intellectually relevant

 Not because I think Epstein secretly invented today's agent economy.

 I don't.

 But those concepts have become **central infrastructure problems**.

 MIT's agentic-web work is literally asking how agents establish:

 - identity;
- trust;
- reputation;
- verification;
- payments;
- coordination.  MIT Sloan+1

 That's remarkably close to the old problem Epstein was interested in:

 **How do you model intelligence, trust, reputation and deception in systems involving autonomous agents?**

 The difference is that we're now trying to implement it commercially.

---

 # 11\. The labor problem is potentially bigger than unemployment

 This is another place I think public debate gets it wrong.

 The immediate question is:

 > “Will AI take my job?”

 The deeper question is:

 > **“Who owns the productivity created when AI performs my job?”**

 The IMF's 2026 analysis estimates approximately **$2.7 trillion in annualized labor-cost-equivalent AI gains** in its sample.

 But it finds those gains are disproportionately concentrated in higher-paid occupations.  IMF eLibrary

 The ILO similarly finds substantial task-level productivity gains, but says the gains have not yet translated into broad economy-wide productivity growth; benefits are concentrated in larger, digitally advanced firms.  International Labour Organization

 And its June 2026 review warns about:

 **How do you model intelligence, trust, reputation and deception in systems involving autonomous agents?**

 - inequality;
- reduced opportunities for younger workers;
- worker autonomy;
- job quality;
- changes in work organization.  International Labour Organization

 So the danger isn't necessarily:

 **“robots eliminate everyone.”**

 It could instead be:

 > **AI weakens workers' bargaining power while increasing the productivity of capital.**

 That's a much more plausible near-term problem.

---

 # 12\. Imagine two companies with identical AI

 Company A says:

 > “Our AI saved 20,000 labor hours. Everyone gets a four-day week.”

 Company B says:

 > “Our AI saved 20,000 labor hours. Fire 15% of the workforce.”

 Same technology.

 Completely different society.

 AI doesn't choose.

 **Ownership, labor law, taxation and bargaining power choose.**

 That's why saying “AI will make everyone more productive” isn't sufficient.

 We need to ask:

 > **productive for whom?**

---

 # 13\. There's also an enormous physical extraction problem

 The AI economy looks digital.

 It isn't.

 It consumes:

 - electricity;
- water;
- chips;
- copper;
- transformers;
- land;
- transmission capacity;
- construction;
- cooling;
- natural resources.

 The IEA reports that data-center electricity consumption grew **17% in 2025**, while AI-focused data centers grew about **50%** in electricity use. It projects total data-center electricity demand from roughly **485 TWh in 2025 to 950 TWh in 2030**, with AI-focused consumption tripling.  IEA+1

 And there's an important nuance:

 **AI is getting much more energy-efficient per task.**

 But usage is exploding and new agentic/video/reasoning workloads can be vastly more energy-intensive.

 So efficiency isn't necessarily reducing total consumption.

 This is the classic:

 > **make something cheaper → people use much more of it**

 effect.

---

 # 14\. And now capital is racing ahead of infrastructure

 This is another major concern.

 The IEA says data-center investments have become too large to be funded entirely from corporate balance sheets, meaning capital markets are increasingly necessary for continued buildout. It also says planning systems, grid connections, chip supply and energy equipment are becoming bottlenecks.  IEA

 So you get:

 **Wall Street/private capital**

 ↓

 **AI data centers**

 ↓

 **electricity demand**

 ↓

 **grid investment**

 ↓

 **public infrastructure**

 ↓

 **AI companies**

 ↓

 **private revenue**

 That doesn't automatically mean exploitation.

 But it creates a question governments need to answer:

 > **If public infrastructure is necessary to make private AI assets profitable, what does the public receive in return?**

 That's an enormous policy question.

---

 # 15\. This is why I think “AI extraction” is the right phrase—but we should define it carefully

 I see at least **six extraction channels**.

 ### 1\. Labor extraction

 AI increases output per worker and potentially reduces labor costs.

 ### 2\. Data extraction

 Human activity becomes training material, behavioral information and economic signal.

 ### 3\. Attention extraction

 AI can make persuasion and personalization much more sophisticated.

 ### 4\. Infrastructure extraction

 Electricity, water, land and public infrastructure are converted into computational capacity.

 ### 5\. Intellectual extraction

 Human-created knowledge becomes model capability.

 ### 6\. Capital extraction

 The resulting intelligence becomes an asset owned by investors.

 And the crucial asymmetry is:

 > **The inputs are socially distributed; ownership of the resulting infrastructure is comparatively concentrated.**

 That's the part that worries me.

---

 # 16\. There's an even deeper problem: AI could make inequality self-reinforcing

 Consider a wealthy organization.

 It has:

 **capital → compute → data → AI → productivity → more capital**

 A poor organization has:

 **less capital → less compute → less data → weaker AI → lower productivity → less capital**

 That is a feedback loop.

 The IMF specifically warns about concentrated compute and frontier-model dependencies creating a resilience gap between AI leaders and laggards.  International Monetary Fund

 So AI could either:

 ### democratize intelligence

 or

 ### concentrate intelligence.

 And **both are technologically possible**.

 The political/economic system determines which one wins.

---

 # 17\. This changes how I interpret the Epstein material

 After digging further, I don't think the most important conclusion is:

 > “Epstein was secretly involved in today's AI.”

 I don't have evidence for that.

 My interpretation is more interesting:

 ### Epstein appears to have recognized something early.

 He was fascinated by the convergence of:

 **intelligence + computation + biology + economics + human behavior + power.**

 He funded people attempting to build general intelligence.

 Some of those researchers are still active.

 Some of the institutions survived.

 And the world has now entered the exact technological transition that those researchers were anticipating.

 That's historically interesting.

 But **Epstein is not necessary to explain the current AI concentration.**

 Market incentives, technological scale economies and capital concentration explain much of it on their own.

---

 # 18\. In fact, I think focusing too much on Epstein could obscure the bigger problem

 This is the biggest adjustment I'd make to our investigation.

 Suppose tomorrow we discovered:

 > Epstein had absolutely no connection whatsoever to modern AI.

 Would the problems disappear?

 No.

 We would still have:

 - concentrated compute;
- concentrated cloud;
- concentrated chips;
- concentrated data;
- massive AI infrastructure spending;
- labor displacement/deskilling risks;
- surveillance;
- autonomous agents;
- energy consumption;
- private ownership of frontier intelligence;
- financialization of compute.

  Those are the issues citizens need to worry about.

 So I would now treat Epstein as **one historical window into an early elite interest in machine intelligence**, not the central explanation.

---

 # 19\. What I think is actually happening

 My best model is something like this:

 ### Phase 1 — Research

 A relatively small scientific community explores AGI, cognition and machine intelligence.

 ### Phase 2 — Patronage

 Wealthy individuals and institutions fund the research because they recognize its potential.

 Epstein was one such patron.

 ### Phase 3 — Commercialization

 AI becomes economically useful.

 ### Phase 4 — Infrastructure race

 Compute becomes the scarce resource.

 ### Phase 5 — Financialization

 Banks, private equity and institutional investors turn compute/data centers into investable infrastructure.

 We're **here**.

 ### Phase 6 — Agentization

 AI stops merely answering questions and begins acting:

 > negotiate → purchase → trade → hire → schedule → transact → coordinate.

 We're beginning to enter this phase.

 ### Phase 7 — Dependency

 Businesses and individuals become dependent on AI infrastructure.

 This is where the stakes become enormous.

 ### Phase 8 — Potential concentration

 A handful of entities could control the infrastructure through which everyone else accesses machine intelligence.

 **This is the phase I think citizens should be paying attention to.**

---

 # 20\. And there is a fork in the road

 I don't think the future is predetermined.

 There are two very different possibilities.

 ## The good version

 AI becomes something like electricity or the Internet.

 Competition remains strong.

 Open models remain viable.

 Public institutions develop their own compute.

 People retain ownership/control over their data.

 Workers capture part of productivity gains.

 AI reduces working hours.

 Energy costs are properly allocated.

 Agents operate on open standards.

 People can move their AI identities/data between providers.

 AI becomes an extraordinary public productivity multiplier.

 ### That's entirely possible.

---

 # 21\. The bad version

 AI becomes more like a private utility controlled by a handful of corporations.

 You rent:

 - intelligence;
- memory;
- identity;
- software;
- financial services;
- healthcare navigation;
- education;
- employment tools.

 Your agent becomes indispensable.

 But the provider controls the underlying infrastructure.

 Your data becomes an asset.

 Your behavior becomes a prediction model.

 Your labor becomes an optimization target.

 Your electricity system subsidizes data-center expansion.

 Your government becomes dependent on private AI infrastructure.

 Your employer uses AI to monitor and optimize you.

 And a relatively small number of capital owners collect the resulting productivity gains.

 **That is a plausible dystopian pathway without requiring a conspiracy.**

---

 # 22\. And this is the part that I think deserves the most attention

 There's a fundamental question emerging:

 > **Should intelligence itself be treated as private property?**

 Not necessarily individual intelligence.

 I'm talking about **machine intelligence as infrastructure**.

 Imagine if one company controlled most of:

 - the world's search;
- its cloud;
- its operating systems;
- its personal assistants;
- its AI models;
- its agent protocols;
- its payments;
- its identity system.

 That's not simply a successful company.

 That's a **quasi-governmental layer of infrastructure**.

 And governments are already struggling to regulate ordinary social platforms.

 AI could be much more consequential because it doesn't just distribute information.

 It can **act.**

---

 # 23\. That's why the agent question may be more important than AGI

 People obsess over:

 > “Will AGI become conscious?”

 I think the more practical question is:

 > **“Who controls the agents?”**

 An agent doesn't need consciousness to radically change society.

 It only needs to be able to:

 - access your bank account;
- negotiate contracts;
- make purchases;
- manage investments;
- communicate;
- control machines;
- interact with other agents.

 MIT researchers are already thinking about the infrastructure required for exactly this world.  MIT Sloan+1

 If those rails are privately controlled, **economic power becomes programmable.**

 That is a much bigger issue than whether a chatbot is sentient.

---

 # 24\. So how concerned am I?

   > **If public infrastructure is necessary to make private AI assets profitable, what does the public receive in return?**

 # 15\. This is why I think “AI extraction” is the right phrase—but we should define it carefully

 I'd revise my earlier assessment upward somewhat.

 ### Epstein/AI conspiracy:

 **2/10**

 I don't have evidence for one.

 ### Epstein's historical AI network:

 **6/10 interesting**

 There is substantially more real continuity than I initially appreciated.

 ### Concentration of AI infrastructure:

 **8/10 concerning**

 The OECD is independently warning about exactly this.  OECD+1

 ### Financialization of compute:

 **8/10 concerning**

 It's happening explicitly, at enormous scale.  NVIDIA Blog+1

 ### Labor/distribution:

 **8/10 concerning**

 The productivity gains are real, but distribution is already uneven.  International Labour Organization+1

 ### Environmental/infrastructure externalities:

 **7/10**

 The physical buildout is enormous and accelerating.  IEA+1

 ### Agentic economy:

 **9/10 potential significance**

 Not because it is necessarily bad, but because it could fundamentally change how economic decisions are made.

---

 # My overall conclusion

 I don't think the deepest story here is:

 > **“Epstein had an AI plan and someone continued it.”**

 I think the deeper story is:

 > **A small community of researchers and wealthy patrons began treating machine intelligence as an extremely powerful general-purpose capability. Over the following decades, that research escaped into commercial systems. Now enormous amounts of capital are converging on the infrastructure required to industrialize intelligence.**

 And we're reaching the point where the question changes from:

 > **“Can machines think?”**

 to:

 > **“Who owns the machines that think, who controls their objectives, whose data feeds them, who gets their productivity, and who has the power to say no?”**

 That is a **real democratic problem**, regardless of Epstein.

 And the most unsettling thing I've found is that the technology is advancing **faster than the institutions needed to govern its distribution**. The IMF explicitly says AI diffusion is outpacing government readiness; the OECD is warning about concentration and vertical integration; the ILO is documenting uneven gains; and the IEA is documenting the physical infrastructure race.  International Monetary Fund+3

 So I wouldn't tell people to panic.

 I'd tell them to **pay very close attention to ownership, infrastructure, labor bargaining power, data rights, agent identity/payment standards, competition policy, and who pays for the physical buildout.**

 Those are the levers.

 And if we continue this investigation, **that's where I'd go next—not deeper into celebrity names, but into the money and ownership graph**: who owns the compute, who finances it, who owns the data centers, who gets government subsidies/contracts, who owns the models, which people sit across multiple boards, and where the productivity gains actually end up.

 That would let us move from _“something feels off”_ to a much more concrete answer about **where power is accumulating and who is paying for it.**

 > open source alone isn't enough.

 Training and operating serious models requires enormous amounts of **compute, data and infrastructure**, which remain concentrated among a small number of actors. ---

 # And there's a subtle clue in the standards work

 The ITU recently described what it thinks an acceptable agent architecture should look like:

 An agent should carry evidence connecting it to the person or organization that delegated its authority, specify **what it is allowed to do and for how long**, and produce auditable evidence of its actions. Authority should be **traceable and revocable**.  ITU

 That is almost the opposite of an opaque corporate AI assistant.

 It says:

 > **The machine doesn't get a blank check.**

 That's a very healthy principle.

---

 # But there's a danger hiding even inside the solution

 Here's where I think we need to be careful.

 ### “Identity”

 sounds benign.

 ### “Trust”

 sounds benign.

 ### “Reputation”

 sounds benign.

 ### “Authorization”

 sounds benign.

 But together they can create something enormously powerful.

 Imagine every AI agent eventually has:

 - a permanent identity;
- a reputation score;
- a transaction history;
- a behavioral history;
- a trust rating;
- access permissions;
- financial relationships.

 That's useful for security.

 But it can also become a **machine-readable social hierarchy**.

 And suddenly you have to ask:

 > Who determines reputation?

 > Who can lower it?

 > Can you appeal?

 > Can an algorithm blacklist you?

 > Can you switch providers?

 > Is your reputation portable?

 > Can your AI agent make mistakes that permanently affect you?

 > Who owns the behavioral record?

 This is why I think **reputation** is actually more important than I initially realized.

 Not because Epstein talked about it.

 Because **the agent economy genuinely needs it.**

 And whoever controls that layer could possess enormous power.

---

 # I also found a genuine technical dead end

 There's an academic paper from 2026 specifically examining AI-agent identity.

 Its conclusion is essentially:

 **we don't have this solved.**

 The authors identify unresolved problems around:

 - verifying what an agent actually is;
- proving what it was authorized to do;
- recursive delegation;
- identity integrity;
- governance opacity;
- enforcement.  arXiv

 That's significant.

 Because we're beginning to give AI agents real-world authority **before society has fully solved how to establish responsibility for their actions**.

 That's not an Epstein problem.

 That's a civilization-scale engineering/governance problem.

 # And that gives us a much better question

 I think the question you've been circling around is actually:

 > **“Who is building the infrastructure that determines how humans and AI will trust, transact, identify and govern one another?”**

 That's much better than:

 > “Who did Epstein know?”

 Because now we can trace **institutions rather than personalities**.

 ### Open-source models

 **Promising.**

 Potential solution to compute concentration.

 ### Worker-owned AI

 **Promising.**

 **Potentially transformative.**

 **Probably the biggest inflection point.**

 Because AI moves from _producing information_ to _possessing authority_.
