Done — the Massachusetts branch is in as its own ledger file (21 entries), with Part 13 pointing to it. A few things from my own checking:

- EO 658 is real and well-documented — multiple outlets confirm the >25 MW threshold, the community-benefits gate, the clean-energy-or-pay rule, and the NDA ban. That's confirmed, not just reported.
- Useful context I found: Massachusetts doesn't actually have many large data centers planned yet. Healey's team calls it preemptive. That makes the state a policy lab — rules written before the load arrives, which is when they matter most.
- One thing I could not independently confirm yet: the specific September 24 DPU order telling the three utilities to disclose their interconnection queues. The EO's DPU directives are confirmed, but that particular order needs a primary pull. It's flagged in the file.
- The Pressley item is the sleeper — the $33.4B AES acquisition puts BlackRock's infrastructure arm on both sides of our map: financing AI infrastructure nationally while buying the utility whose ratepayers might absorb data-center costs.

This stays separate from your Auditor-anchored Massachusetts work — different subject, different file.

 # Massachusetts AI / Data-Center Accountability Ledger — v1

 **Research cutoff: October 5, 2026**

 ## A. Money already on the books

 ### 1\. Massachusetts data-center tax exemption — **$17 million/year**

 **Date:** Effective for qualifying purchases made after **November 20, 2024**\
 **Law:** M.G.L. c. 64H §6(zz)\
 **Benefit:** Sales/use-tax exemption for:

 - qualifying data-center equipment;
- software;
- electricity consumed by the data center;
- construction, renovation and refurbishment costs.

 Massachusetts requires a qualified facility to have at least **100,000 square feet**, incur at least **$50 million** in qualified costs over the relevant period, and maintain at least **100 Massachusetts jobs**. Certification can last **20 years**.  Massachusetts Government+1

 The Governor's FY2026 tax-expenditure budget estimated the value of this exemption at **$17.0 million** for FY2025 and **$17.0 million** for FY2026. The budget explicitly says its estimates are in millions of dollars.  Massachusetts Budget+1

 **Ledger classification:** `TAX EXPENDITURE / ESTIMATE`

 **Important:** $17M is **not money paid to data centers**. It is the state's estimated foregone sales-tax revenue associated with the exemption.

---

 ### 2\. Healey pauses the exemption

 **Date:** **June 25, 2026**\
 **Person:** Gov. Maura Healey\
 **Action:** Massachusetts paused acceptance of new applications for the data-center sales/use-tax exemption.

 Healey said the pause was intended to put stronger protections in place for ratepayers, the environment and public health.  Massachusetts Government

 The September executive order subsequently incorporated the June action into the state's formal data-center framework.  Massachusetts Government

 **Ledger classification:** `POLICY CHANGE`

 ### What we still need

 This is a major records request:

 > **How many companies had applied before the June 25 cutoff, what were their proposed investments, and how much tax expenditure would each project have generated?**

 That could convert the $17M statewide estimate into a **company-by-company ledger**.

---

 # B. The $1 billion Massachusetts energy bill

 ### 3\. H.5175 — proposed $1 billion ratepayer savings

 **Date introduced:** **February 26, 2026**\
 **Bill:** H.5175\
 **Subject:** Energy affordability, clean power and economic competitiveness

 The bill would require utilities and municipal aggregators to modify their energy-efficiency programs to produce **at least $1 billion in aggregate ratepayer savings**.  Massachusetts Legislature

 It also requires each electric company to file a tariff specifically for supplying electricity to data centers.

 **Ledger classification:** `PROPOSED SAVINGS / PROPOSED REGULATION`

 **Do not count $1B as money recovered.** It is a statutory target contained in proposed legislation.

---

 # C. Rodney Elliott's data-center cost bill

 ### 4\. Rep. Rodney M. Elliott — HD.5404

 **Date:** Filed in 2025; referred to House Rules **December 11, 2025**\
 **Sponsor:** Rep. Rodney M. Elliott of Lowell

 The bill defines a large/qualified data center as one capable of using **10 MW or more**.

 It would prohibit utilities from increasing residential or income-eligible customer rates because of large data centers.

 More importantly for our investigation, it would require utilities to provide regulators with:

 ## A. Money already on the books

 - cost-attribution records;
- data-center interconnection/build-out agreements;
- electricity-sales records related to data centers.

  Massachusetts Legislature+1  **Ledger classification:** `PROPOSED TRANSPARENCY / RATEPAYER PROTECTION`

 This is one of our best examples of a Massachusetts legislator trying to obtain the exact financial records we're looking for.

 # F. The $325.1M / $100M economic-development bill

 Here's a number we need to keep in the ledger, but **not falsely attribute to AI**.

- **$100 million in direct FY2026 appropriations**

 The Massachusetts Senate amendment record explicitly gives those figures.  Massachusetts Legislature

 It is a broader economic-development package.

 # G. Healey Executive Order 658

 ### 7\. The major regulatory event

 **Date:** **September 8, 2026**\
 **Governor:** Maura Healey\
 **Executive Order:** **658**

 The order applies heightened requirements to data centers exceeding **25 MW peak demand**.

 Before receiving relevant permits, they must demonstrate compliance with Massachusetts's data-center framework and submit a community-benefits agreement.  Massachusetts Government

 The order specifically directs the DPU to develop large-load rate schedules designed so that:

 > other ratepayers don't pay for distribution-grid upgrades or associated infrastructure needed for data-center demand.

  Massachusetts Government  That's a critical piece of our investigation.

 ### Clean-energy requirement

 For \>25 MW projects, MassDEP is directed to establish protocols requiring enough **incremental new clean electricity generation** to cover annual consumption.

 If a data center doesn't procure enough clean electricity, the state is directed to establish an **alternative compliance payment** mechanism by **December 31, 2026**.

 The collected money goes into a **Ratepayer Protection Fund**.  Massachusetts Government

 **Dollar amount:** Not established yet.

 **But the mechanism is important.**

 It creates a future equation:

 > data-center electricity shortfall → compliance payment → ratepayer-protection fund.

 # What jumps out

 There are **four numbers I would put in giant type on the investigation board**:

 ### **$17 million**

 Estimated annual Massachusetts sales-tax expenditure for the qualified-data-center exemption.  Massachusetts Budget

 ### **$1 billion**

 Minimum aggregate ratepayer savings contemplated by H.5175's energy-efficiency provisions. **Proposed—not realized savings.**  Massachusetts Legislature

 ### **25 MW**

 The threshold that now triggers major additional state requirements under Healey's EO 658.  Massachusetts Government

 ### **10 MW**

 The threshold used by Rep. Elliott's HD.5404 for its proposed ratepayer protections.  Massachusetts Legislature

 Those thresholds matter because they define **which projects get treated differently**.

 | Facility | City | Developer | Owner | AI/customer | MW | Utility | Interconnection cost | Tax exemption | Property tax | State subsidy | Local subsidy | Water | Jobs |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |

And then cross-reference every facility against the **monthly Eversource, National Grid and Unitil interconnection files** that Massachusetts has already published.  Massachusetts Government

 That is where I expect we'll start getting the **big dollar amounts** you're looking for—actual projects rather than statewide policy estimates.

 One other thing I would specifically investigate is **Western Massachusetts**, because the state data can tell us whether projects in/around the region are creating transmission, generation, land-use or ratepayer exposure. I would not assume there is a connection until the interconnection and permitting records show one.

 This ledger is now sufficiently grounded that we can start assigning every number a **source, date, person/company, legal mechanism, and cash-vs-commitment status** rather than building a pile of scary-looking figures.

[Oct 05, 2026, 6:38 PM EDT] RICKY:

here's some stuff from the other AI:

I did. I followed several threads outward rather than stopping at the obvious “AI/data centers are expensive” story.

 The picture that emerges is **more complicated—and in a few places more interesting—than the earlier ledger suggested.** I also found several apparent leads that currently go nowhere, which is important because it keeps us from forcing everything into one theory.

 ## The biggest thing I found

 There are actually **two Massachusetts AI systems developing simultaneously**:

 1. **A public-interest AI infrastructure system** — universities, MGHPCC, state grants, applied research, workforce development.
2. **A commercial AI/data-center system** — private developers, enormous electricity loads, tax incentives, utilities, rate structures and private capital.

 They overlap politically and institutionally, but **I have not found evidence that they are secretly one operation**.

 The interesting question is whether the public system is effectively helping build the ecosystem that private AI subsequently monetizes.

 That's a much more defensible question.

---

 # 1\. The public money trail is larger than our first ledger showed

 ### May 6, 2025 — $31 million

 Gov. Maura Healey announced a **$31 million state grant** to MGHPCC for the Artificial Intelligence Compute Resources project.

 The state says the project will eventually involve approximately **$120 million in joint investment** over five years from Massachusetts and MGHPCC's six universities:

 - Boston University
- Harvard
- MIT
- Northeastern
- UMass
- Yale

 The system is being built with Cambridge Computer, Dell, NVIDIA and VAST Data.  Massachusetts Government+1

 This is important because the state isn't merely subsidizing AI research.

 It's helping purchase **actual AI compute infrastructure**.

 And the hardware includes **hundreds of NVIDIA B200 and RTX GPUs**.  Massachusetts AI Hub

 ### September 29, 2026

 MGHPCC announced the AI Compute Resource as a shared statewide platform for AI research, education, entrepreneurship and commercialization.  MGHPCC

 So:

 **taxpayer/state capital → compute → researchers/startups → commercial AI**

 That's a legitimate economic-development strategy.

 But it is also exactly the kind of capital flow we should quantify.

---

 The Massachusetts capital budget lists:

 ### Applied AI Hub

 # 4\. But then we hit an interesting procurement thread

 This one deserves a red flag—not because I found wrongdoing, but because the documentation raises questions.

 Massachusetts decided to deploy ChatGPT across roughly **40,000 executive-branch employees**.

 The procurement was:

 **RFQ 26-04261**

 The state solicited bids beginning in September 2025.  CommBuys

 On **January 27, 2026**, Massachusetts named:

 ### Carahsoft Technology Corporation

 as the **apparent successful bidder**.  WBUR Media

 The underlying response identifies the offer as:

 > **OpenAI (via Carahsoft)**

 And the pricing submitted was:

 | Seats | Annual OpenAI platform fee |
| --- | --- |
| 10,000 | **$1.56M** |
| 20,000 | **$2.64M** |
| 30,000 | **$3.24M** |
| 40,000 | **$3.36M** |

 Blox Images  So the maximum published platform price is roughly:

 ### **$3.36 million/year**

 for 40,000 seats.

 That's about **$84 per employee per year**, before considering the exact final contract structure.

 And this is an important correction to our earlier picture:

 **The procurement record points to Carahsoft as the contracting bidder/reseller, while the technology is OpenAI.**

 The state's public announcement describes the arrangement as a contract with OpenAI.  Massachusetts Government

---

 # 5\. Now the Carahsoft thread gets interesting

 I followed it.

 Carahsoft is not an obscure startup.

 It has a long history as a government technology reseller.

 More importantly, the Justice Department previously reached a **$75.5 million settlement involving VMware and Carahsoft** concerning allegations that commercial pricing information was concealed and the government was overcharged.

 That case concerned conduct from **2007–2013**, and the settlement resolved allegations without a determination of liability.  Department of Justice

 Separately, DOJ's 2024–25 investigation into federal IT procurement resulted in criminal charges and guilty pleas involving other companies/individuals in bid-rigging and bribery schemes.

 **I did not find evidence that Carahsoft itself was charged in that criminal investigation.**

 That's an important distinction.

 So this:

 > "Carahsoft was involved in government procurement controversy"

 is supported.

 This:

 > "Carahsoft committed the Massachusetts AI procurement fraud"

 is **not supported by what I found**.

---

 # 6\. There is an even more interesting unanswered question

 But Massachusetts activist/state legislator Erika Uyterhoeven has alleged, based on records she obtained, that an AI procurement scoring rubric gave **0% weight to AI safety**.

 She also alleges that both finalists came through Carahsoft.

 That is potentially important—but I am treating it as **an allegation requiring primary-document verification**, not a finding.  Electerika

 The underlying procurement documents are available enough that this is something we can actually check.

 # 7\. The state simultaneously says "AI safety is essential"

 This creates an interesting tension.

 Massachusetts says the ChatGPT deployment has:

 - secure/walled-off infrastructure;
- data segregation;
- privacy controls;
- retention controls;
- human review;
- security requirements;
- employee training.

 The state explicitly tells employees that ChatGPT can generate incorrect or incomplete answers and that employees remain responsible for verifying outputs.  Massachusetts Government+1

 So the question isn't:

 > "Does Massachusetts care about AI safety?"

 It clearly does.

 The more useful question is:

 > **Did the actual procurement evaluation enforce the same safety standards that the administration publicly claims to value?**

 That is a factual question we can answer by examining the procurement scoring documents.

---

 # 8\. Now follow the infrastructure money

 This is where the Massachusetts story becomes substantially more interesting.

 Healey's September 8 Executive Order 658 says:

 > data centers above **25 MW** must bear their energy/infrastructure costs rather than shifting them to other ratepayers.

 It also directs the DPU to remove **speculative data-center projects from utility interconnection queues**.  Massachusetts Government

 Then on **September 24, 2026**, DPU sent orders directly to:

 - Eversource
- National Grid
- Unitil

  It clearly does.

  > **Did the actual procurement evaluation enforce the same safety standards that the administration publicly claims to value?**

 That is a factual question we can answer by examining the procurement scoring documents.

---

 # 8\. Now follow the infrastructure money

 This is where the Massachusetts story becomes substantially more interesting.

  > data centers above **25 MW** must bear their energy/infrastructure costs rather than shifting them to other ratepayers.

  Then on **September 24, 2026**, DPU sent orders directly to:

 - Eversource
- National Grid
- Unitil

  - every \>25 MW load project;
- which are data centers;
- every data center \>1 MW;
- queue position;
- current status;
- estimated interconnection cost;
- estimated timeline.  Massachusetts Government

 This is huge.

 Because previously we were asking:

 > "Are data centers shifting costs?"

 Now the state is collecting the records needed to answer it.

---

 # 9\. Massachusetts already publishes utility interconnection data

 This is the part I would **pull next before chasing politicians further**.

 The Commonwealth publishes monthly raw interconnection data from:

 - National Grid
- Eversource East
- Eversource West
- Unitil

 The public archive currently goes through **August 2026**.  Massachusetts Government

 So we can potentially build a facility-level database rather than rely on press releases.

 That's where the investigation becomes empirical.

---

 # 10\. Then I found the Holyoke thread

 This one is particularly revealing because it provides a **small-scale comparison case**.

 Chestnut River Power & Infrastructure proposed a:

 ### **20 MW**

 ### **$200 million**

 ### data center

 ### at 100 Water Street, Holyoke.

 The proposal was for redevelopment of the former Hampden Papers complex.  WAMC+1

 The developer said it would use closed-loop cooling and Holyoke hydropower and would be designed to minimize municipal utility impacts.  WAMC

 And here's the fascinating part.

 The project is **below Healey's new 25 MW threshold**.

 So it would not automatically trigger the full EO 658 regime simply by being a 20 MW facility.

 That's not necessarily a loophole—the order's threshold is explicit—but it is precisely the kind of boundary condition an investigator should examine.

---

 On **June 16, 2026**, Holyoke's City Council voted:

 ### **9–4**

 to prohibit new data centers, while exempting the existing MGHPCC facility. The mayor subsequently signed off on the restrictions.  WAMC

 And the developer said it would continue looking elsewhere for former industrial sites where:

 ### data center

---

 # 11\. Holyoke rejected it anyway

  ### **9–4**

   - power already exists;
- zoning permits development;
- vacant industrial buildings can be reused;
- the local tax base needs investment.

  NEPM  That gives us a potentially important **development model**:

 > distressed industrial property + existing power infrastructure + local need for tax revenue + data-center redevelopment.

 That's not inherently bad.

 In fact, economically it can make a lot of sense.

 But it's worth following because **those are exactly the communities most likely to compete for data-center development.**

---

 # 12\. And the proposed Holyoke economics are striking

 The mayor's office said the project could potentially generate approximately:

 ### **$2 million/year in property-tax revenue**

 according to WAMC's reporting.  WAMC

 So the basic pitch was:

 **$200M private redevelopment**

 → **20 MW**

 → potential **$2M/year local property tax**

 → reuse contaminated/industrial land

 → use existing hydropower.

 That is an entirely different economic proposition from a 1–10 GW hyperscale AI campus.

 This is why I don't think we should treat every data center as one thing.

---

 # 13\. The existing Holyoke facility is an important control group

 MGHPCC is fascinating because it gives us a real-world example of a comparatively sustainable compute facility.

 WBUR reports:

 > distressed industrial property \+ existing power infrastructure \+ local need for tax revenue + data-center redevelopment.

---

 # 12\. And the proposed Holyoke economics are striking

  ### **$2 million/year in property-tax revenue**

  **$200M private redevelopment**

    → use existing hydropower.

    - up to **12 MW** permitted grid draw;
- actual usage below that;
- **20,000–30,000 gallons/day** of water;
- approximately **90% carbon-free local electricity**;
- recycled cooling water;
- monthly diesel-generator testing;
- highest USGBC energy-efficiency rating received in 2013.  WBUR

 It also serves:

 - Harvard
- MIT
- UMass
- Northeastern
- Boston University
- Yale.

 This is potentially our **control case**.

 We can ask:

 > What does an environmentally efficient data center cost per MW?

 versus:

 > What does a commercial AI data center cost per MW?

 That could tell us whether the environmental burden is technologically necessary—or largely a consequence of how quickly and cheaply commercial operators want to build.

---

 # 14\. Here's another public-money connection

 Massachusetts' AI Hub says at least **40% of AICR compute time** is intended for startups, entrepreneurs and non-member colleges/nonprofits statewide.  Massachusetts AI Hub

 That's significant.

 Because it means the state's model isn't simply:

 > subsidize Harvard/MIT.

 It's explicitly attempting to democratize access to expensive compute.

 Again, this is evidence **against** the simplest extraction theory.

---

 # 15\. But there's a deeper structural issue

 Look at the sequence:

 ### 2024

 Massachusetts authorizes **$25M** for AI projects improving government operations and digital services through the FutureTech Act.  Massachusetts Government

 ### December 2024

 Healey establishes the Massachusetts AI Hub.

 ### February 2025

 AI Models Innovation Challenge launches.

 ### May 2025

 **$31M** state AI-compute grant.

 ### October 2025

 **$2.882M** awarded to seven applied-AI projects.

 ### February 2026

 State announces enterprise ChatGPT procurement for \~40,000 employees.

 ### February 2026

 Google partnership gives Massachusetts residents free AI training.

 ### May 2026

 IBM/Red Hat Open Accelerator launches.

 ### June 2026

 Massachusetts pauses its data-center tax exemption.

 ### September 2026

 Healey establishes strict data-center requirements.

 ### September 2026

 DPU starts demanding detailed data-center interconnection information.

 That's a very clear evolution.

 It looks less like an accidental collection of programs and more like a **deliberate state industrial policy for AI**.

 That is not inherently sinister.

 It is actually what governments routinely do with strategic industries.

 But it means we should stop asking:

 > "Is government involved in AI?"

 The answer is obviously **yes**.

 The better question is:

 > **Who captures the upside from the public investment, and who carries the downside?**

---

 # 16\. And there is a particularly interesting asymmetry

 Look at the two sides.

 ### Massachusetts is willing to disclose:

 - public grants;
- program goals;
- AI Hub investments;
- data-center rules;
- interconnection requirements;
- environmental requirements.

 But some of the most consequential private numbers can remain difficult to obtain:

 - utility contracts;
- negotiated rates;
- security deposits;
- infrastructure cost-sharing;
- private financing;
- actual data-center power purchases.

 That's exactly the information Warren has been trying to obtain nationally.  Senator Elizabeth Warren+1

 And Massachusetts's own DPU has now recognized the problem by demanding much more information from the utilities.

---

 # 17\. There is a real "race" incentive

 The Holyoke MGHPCC story gives us a clue.

 John Goodhue told WBUR that the facility could make environmentally efficient choices partly because **it wasn't under the same pressure to build rapidly**.

 The commercial AI industry is operating under a different incentive:

 > build compute now or somebody else gets ahead.

 WBUR quoted a Penn/Google researcher describing this as a fear of missing the AI race.  WBUR

 That may be one of the most important mechanisms in the whole investigation.

 Not conspiracy.

 **Competition.**

 Competition can cause companies to rationally make decisions that are collectively expensive.

---

 # 18\. And now we have the national connection

 Warren's March 2026 letter to EIA is almost a blueprint for our ledger.

 She and Sen. Josh Hawley want the government to collect:

 - hourly electricity consumption;
- annual consumption;
- peak consumption;
- electricity prices paid;
- upfront payments;
- security deposits;
- demand-response arrangements;
- AI-server electricity versus ordinary cloud workloads;
- transmission costs;
- distribution costs;
- **who bears those costs**.

  Senator Elizabeth Warren  That is basically the dataset we need.

 And EIA subsequently announced plans for mandatory data-center surveys.  Senator Elizabeth Warren

 # 19\. One thing I _didn't_ find

 **AI company → politician → campaign contribution → data-center approval → utility benefit**

 I **didn't find one that I can responsibly present as established**.

 That's important.

 There are lots of tech-sector political relationships.

 There are lots of AI investments.

 There are lots of government partnerships.

 But I don't yet have evidence tying those together into a corrupt transaction.

 So I'm marking that thread:

 ### **UNPROVEN**

 rather than filling the gap with speculation.

 # 20\. Another dead end: Epstein

 This Massachusetts investigation has **not produced a meaningful Epstein connection**.

 That's actually useful.

 The Massachusetts AI ecosystem we're uncovering is contemporary and institutionally explainable:

 **Healey → MassTech → AI Hub → universities → NVIDIA/Dell/VAST → utilities → data centers → private developers → OpenAI/Google/IBM/etc.**

 I don't currently have evidence that Epstein's old AI relationships are controlling or directing this system.

 Trying to force that connection would weaken the investigation.

---

 # 21\. What actually concerns me most

 Not a secret cabal.

 Not Epstein.

 Not even AI itself.

 It's this:

 ### **The speed of capital deployment is outrunning the government's ability to measure who pays for the infrastructure.**

 You can see Massachusetts reacting to that in real time.

 The state:

  1. created AI incentives;
2. built public compute infrastructure;
3. encouraged private AI development;
4. granted/created a data-center tax exemption;
5. began receiving data-center proposals;
6. then paused the tax incentive;
7. established a \>25 MW regulatory threshold;
8. created a ratepayer-protection mechanism;
9. ordered utilities to identify projects and costs.

 That sequence is extremely informative.  Massachusetts Government+2

 It looks like policymakers themselves realized:

 > **"We need better accounting before this gets too big."**

 # My current assessment

---

 After following the threads, I would **not** characterize what I'm seeing as evidence of a hidden Epstein-style AI operation.

 I _would_ characterize it as a rapidly forming **AI political-economy** with a very recognizable structure:

 > **government capital → research infrastructure → private technology ecosystem → enormous private compute investment → electricity demand → utility investment → potential ratepayer exposure → government attempts to catch up with regulation.**

 And Massachusetts gives us something unusually valuable:

 **a relatively small jurisdiction where the entire chain may be traceable.**

 The Holyoke example makes this particularly useful because we have a **public-interest compute facility, a rejected private 20-MW proposal, a $200M claimed investment, a potential $2M/year tax benefit, public AI infrastructure worth $31M+, and a state government simultaneously tightening the rules around commercial data centers.**  WAMC+2

 That's a much more interesting investigation than simply "AI is bad."

 And there is one number I want more than almost any other:

 ## **The total Massachusetts utility/interconnection bill attributable to data centers.**

 Once we have that, we can compare it against:

 **tax revenue + jobs \+ private investment + public subsidies + electricity revenue + environmental costs.**

 Then we can finally ask, with numbers rather than ideology:

 > **Who is actually winning, who is paying, and where does the money go?**

Yes. **A substantial amount of this is researchable from public records**, and the material I just checked confirms that several of the most important pieces are not hypothetical—they are records we can systematically pull.

 The limitation is not “we can't research it.” The limitation is that **some of the most important financial information is not yet public, is buried in attachments/spreadsheets/PDFs, or requires records requests**.

 ### What I can actually build

 I can work through this as a records-based investigation and assemble a ledger with:

 - **People** — governors, legislators, regulators, utility executives, developers, investors, vendors, university officials.
- **Companies** — data-center developers, utilities, AI companies, contractors, equipment vendors.
- **Projects** — facility, location, MW, developer, status, proposed investment.
- **Dates** — legislation, applications, permits, interconnection requests, awards, contracts, approvals.
- **Dollar amounts** — grants, tax expenditures, proposed investments, contracts, utility/grid costs, property-tax estimates, subsidies.
- **Electricity** — MW requested, queue position, interconnection costs and timelines.
- **Public money** — appropriations, grants, tax exemptions and economic-development programs.
- **Political money** — campaign contributions and lobbying where records permit.
- **Procurement** — bids, evaluation criteria, scores, contracts and vendors.
- **Ownership** — corporate entities and relationships where public records establish them.
- **Environmental costs** — electricity, water, emissions and infrastructure requirements.
- **Outcomes** — what was actually approved versus merely proposed.

 And there is a particularly important development: Massachusetts regulations **require qualified data centers to file annual reports under penalty of perjury** containing actual jobs, actual investment and actual exempt expenditures. EOED can also require accountant-certified cost accounting.  Massachusetts Government+1

 That's exactly the kind of primary-source material we want.

 ### The utility side is also unusually promising

 Massachusetts has publicly available monthly interconnection datasets from **National Grid, Eversource East, Eversource West and Unitil**. The state currently has data through July 2026 plus August reports for the individual utilities.  Massachusetts Government

 And the DPU has now explicitly ordered those utilities to identify:

  - **People** — governors, legislators, regulators, utility executives, developers, investors, vendors, university officials.
- **Companies** — data-center developers, utilities, AI companies, contractors, equipment vendors.
- **Projects** — facility, location, MW, developer, status, proposed investment.
- **Dates** — legislation, applications, permits, interconnection requests, awards, contracts, approvals.
- **Dollar amounts** — grants, tax expenditures, proposed investments, contracts, utility/grid costs, property-tax estimates, subsidies.
- **Electricity** — MW requested, queue position, interconnection costs and timelines.
- **Public money** — appropriations, grants, tax exemptions and economic-development programs.
- **Political money** — campaign contributions and lobbying where records permit.
- **Procurement** — bids, evaluation criteria, scores, contracts and vendors.
- **Ownership** — corporate entities and relationships where public records establish them.
- **Environmental costs** — electricity, water, emissions and infrastructure requirements.
- **Outcomes** — what was actually approved versus merely proposed.

  ### The utility side is also unusually promising

  - **every \>25 MW load project** in their queues;
- every **\>1 MW data center**;
- queue position;
- current status;
- estimated interconnection cost;
- estimated timeline.

  Massachusetts Government  That's an extraordinary opportunity because it gives us a route from:

 **project → MW → utility → grid cost → developer → ownership → tax treatment.**

 ### The AI procurement is researchable too

 The Massachusetts ChatGPT procurement isn't just a press release.

 The actual COMMBUYS solicitation record contains the RFQ, questions and answers, award notice, cloud terms, statement-of-work template, risk-management terms and other attachments for **RFQ 26-04261**.  CommBuys

 And the Commonwealth's current description says proposals were evaluated on security/privacy, usability/accessibility, training/support, licensing flexibility and responsible-AI practices.  Massachusetts Government

 So we can test claims about the procurement against the **actual scoring documents**, rather than relying on political statements or press coverage.

 ### The tax side is unusually tractable

 The law requires an applicant to identify the owner/operator and facility, and the exemption can last **20 years**. A facility can lose certification if it fails the $50 million investment or 100-job requirements.  Massachusetts Government+1

 That gives us a concrete question:

 > **Which companies actually received the exemption, how much did they invest, how many jobs did they create, and how much sales tax did Massachusetts forgo?**

 That's far better than simply quoting the statewide $17 million estimate.

---

 ## What I cannot simply find on the internet

 There are some hard boundaries.

 **Private contracts:** A establish who did what, when and for how much. They generally can't establish that somebody secretly intended data center may negotiate electricity prices or infrastructure contributions privately.

 **Beneficial ownership:** Some corporate structures require SEC filings, state corporate records or property records to untangle.

 **Campaign influence:** We can identify contributions and lobbying, but a contribution does not prove a quid pro quo.

 **Actual utility cost allocation:** Some of this may only emerge through DPU proceedings, confidential filings or public-records requests.

 **Unreleased applications:** If EOED hasn't published a particular data-center application or annual report, we can't pretend we have it.

 **Intent:** Documents can establish who did what, when and for how much. They generally can't establish that somebody secretly intended a particular outcome without evidence.

 Those distinctions will be **built into the ledger** rather than hidden.

---

 # And there's one thing I'd change about our investigation

 I wouldn't make the central hypothesis:

 > “There is a hidden AI/Epstein network.”

 The evidence I've found so far doesn't justify that.

 I'd make the investigation much harder to dismiss:

 > **How is the emerging AI economy actually financed, who receives the benefits, who bears the infrastructure and social costs, and what relationships connect the public institutions to the private beneficiaries?**

 Then Epstein becomes **one historical thread that can be tested**, rather than the assumption that explains everything.

 If the evidence connects it, we'll show the connection.

 If it doesn't, we'll document the dead end.

 That's how we avoid accidentally constructing a conspiracy theory out of coincidental relationships.

 The next stage I'd tackle is **not another general web search**.

 I'd build the actual Massachusetts database from the primary records:

 The public records are sufficiently rich that **yes, I can take this substantially further**. The DPU interconnection files and the qualified-data-center annual reporting requirements are probably the two highest-value veins to mine first.  Massachusetts Government+2

Yes. I went another layer down, and I think the investigation is getting more useful. The strongest findings are **not** evidence of a single hidden conspiracy. They are evidence of several concrete systems interacting—public money, AI vendors, government procurement, electricity infrastructure, tax policy, universities, and private development—and there are specific places where the financial accountability is still incomplete.

 ## 1\. The most important new finding: Massachusetts is building both sides of the AI economy

 The Commonwealth isn't merely regulating AI.

 It is simultaneously **subsidizing the infrastructure needed to create it**.

 On May 6, 2025, Healey announced a **$31 million state grant** to MGHPCC for the Artificial Intelligence Compute Resources project. The six university members—Boston University, Harvard, MIT, Northeastern, UMass and Yale—are expected to provide matching investment, with total joint investment expected to exceed **$120 million by 2030**.  Massachusetts Government+1

 The system is being built with:

 - Cambridge Computer
- Dell Technologies
- VAST Data
- NVIDIA

 and will contain hundreds of NVIDIA B200 and RTX GPUs.  MassTech

 This isn't abstract AI policy.

 It's **public capital buying physical compute infrastructure**.

 And there's an interesting geographic component: the infrastructure is in **Holyoke**, powered by Holyoke Gas & Electric's hydroelectric resources, with the state describing it as 100% carbon-free power.  Massachusetts AI Hub

 ### The question I'd now ask

 Who ultimately receives the $31 million?

 Not merely "Cambridge Computer."

 We want:

 **$31M → purchase orders → hardware → software → integration → installation → maintenance → subcontractors → university contribution → ownership of equipment.**

 That's a records-level question.

---

 # 2\. There is a potentially important distinction between public and commercial compute

 MGHPCC is actually a useful control group.

 The state is trying to create a relatively sustainable public/research model:

 > compute + hydroelectric power + universities + startups + public research.

 The commercial data-center model is different:

 > private capital + enormous load + utility interconnection + tax incentives + private customers.

 That distinction matters enormously.

 If public compute can operate using relatively clean existing power infrastructure, then the question becomes:

 **How much of the environmental and grid burden of commercial AI is technologically unavoidable, and how much is caused by the economics of rapid private expansion?**

 That's a question we can actually investigate.

---

 # 3\. The Massachusetts tax exemption is more consequential than the $17M number suggests

 The state's qualified-data-center exemption isn't merely equipment.

 It can cover:

 - equipment;
- software;
- electricity;
- construction;
- renovation/refurbishment.

 And the exemption can last **20 years**.  Massachusetts Government

 A qualifying facility must generally:

   We want:

  > private capital + enormous load + utility interconnection \+ tax incentives + private customers.

 - be at least **100,000 square feet**;
- create/maintain **100 Massachusetts jobs**;
- invest at least **$50 million** in qualifying costs.

  Massachusetts Government+1  The FY2026 budget estimated the annual tax expenditure at **$17 million**.  Massachusetts Budget

 But here's the part I think deserves much more attention:

 ### Certified facilities must report actual numbers every year.

 The required report includes:

 ### Certified facilities must report actual numbers every year.

 - actual jobs;
- actual investment;
- actual exempt expenditures.

 The report must be signed under **penalties of perjury**, and EOED can require an accountant-certified cost accounting.  Massachusetts Government

 That's gold for an investigation.

 Because we don't have to settle for:

 > "The state estimates $17M."

 We can potentially reconstruct:

 > **Company A actually bought $X of equipment, $Y of electricity, $Z of construction, and claimed $N in sales-tax exemptions.**

 That is the number I would chase.

---

 # 4\. And the state has now stopped accepting new applications

 On **June 25, 2026**, EOED stopped accepting new qualified-data-center tax-exemption applications.  Massachusetts Government

 Then on September 8, Healey issued EO 658.

 That sequence is significant:

 **Tax incentive created → data-center development accelerates → state pauses incentive → state imposes stronger requirements.**

 I don't read that as evidence of wrongdoing.

 I read it as evidence that **the administration itself became concerned about the external costs of the development model.**

 EO 658 explicitly says Massachusetts residents shouldn't bear the costs or burdens of data-center development and directs DPU to make sure other ratepayers don't pay for distribution upgrades associated with data-center demand.  Massachusetts Government

 ### Every \>25 MW load project

 The utilities must identify:

 > "The state estimates $17M."

 We can potentially reconstruct:

 > **Company A actually bought $X of equipment, $Y of electricity, $Z of construction, and claimed $N in sales-tax exemptions.**

 That is the number I would chase.

 # 6\. There's a very interesting legislative threshold fight

 Different proposals have used different MW thresholds.

 ### 10 MW

 Rep. Rodney Elliott's HD.5404 would define large/qualified data centers at **10 MW+** and require utilities to provide regulators with cost-attribution and data-center interconnection agreements.  Massachusetts Legislature

 ### 20 MW

 H.5175 defines a data center at **20 MW+** and proposes:

---

 # 6\. There's a very interesting legislative threshold fight

 Different proposals have used different MW thresholds.

 ### 10 MW

  - at least 80% renewable electricity;
- restrictions on fossil backup generation;
- data-center-specific tariffs;
- protection for non-data-center ratepayers.

 It also contains a proposed **$1 billion** aggregate ratepayer savings target.  Massachusetts Legislature

 ### 25 MW

 Healey's EO 658 establishes the major state regulatory threshold at **25 MW**.  Massachusetts Government

 ### 20 MW moratorium

 Sen. Michael Moore, with Sens. Eldridge and Montigny, proposed a moratorium on applications/approvals for **20 MW+** facilities until November 1, 2027, together with a Data Center Coordination Council.  Massachusetts Legislature

 That amendment wasn't adopted.

 ### Why this matters

 A facility's classification can literally depend on whether it's:

 **19.9 MW → 20 MW → 25 MW.**

 That makes the precise project load incredibly important.

 I'd therefore add:

 > **MW threshold engineering**

 to the investigation.

 For every major project, we want the original engineering load forecast—not merely the public number.

---

 # 7\. The proposed tariffs reveal what regulators are afraid of

 One of the most revealing documents is Senate Amendment 193.

 It proposes special data-center electricity tariffs containing things like:

 - minimum charges;
- minimum contract length;
- minimum monthly billing demand;
- collateral requirements;
- mandatory notice periods;
- fees for reducing capacity;
- premature termination fees.

  Massachusetts Legislature  Why?

 Because of **stranded-cost risk**.

 Imagine a developer tells a utility:

 > "We're going to need 300 MW."

 The utility spends hundreds of millions upgrading infrastructure.

 Then the AI market changes, financing collapses, or the developer walks away.

 Someone is left holding the infrastructure.

 The proposed tariff mechanisms are essentially designed to prevent:

 > **private speculative risk → public/other-ratepayer liability.**

 That is one of the clearest explanations of the underlying economic problem I've found.

---

 # 8\. The "speculative project" issue is explicitly acknowledged by the state

 EO 658 directs DPU to eliminate **speculative data-center projects** from interconnection queues.  Massachusetts Government

 That word matters.

 It means the government isn't simply dealing with facilities that already exist.

 Some projects entering the queue may represent:

 - land banking;
- preliminary development;
- power reservation;
- speculative capacity;
- projects without financing;
- projects that may never be constructed.

 Yet grid planning has to account for them.

 That's potentially a major source of distortion.

 ### The question:

 **How many MW are sitting in Massachusetts' interconnection queue that will never materialize?**

 If the answer is enormous, that's an important piece of the story.

 # 3\. The tax exemption is much broader than "data-center equipment"

 This is probably the biggest financial finding so far.

 Massachusetts' statute exempts:

 - data-center equipment;
- software;

- construction costs.

  Massachusetts Government+1
---

 # 3\. The tax exemption is much broader than "data-center equipment"

   - data-center equipment;
- software;
- **electricity consumed by the facility**;
- construction costs.

  And the statutory qualification is:

 **$50 million+ in qualifying costs + 100 Massachusetts jobs + 100,000 square feet**, among other requirements.  Massachusetts Government

 The exemption can continue for **20 years**. It also survives a sale of the facility—the remaining benefit can transfer to a new owner.  Massachusetts Government+1

 That means this isn't simply:

 > "Massachusetts gave a company a break on some servers."

 It potentially encompasses the **electricity and construction economics of an enormous industrial facility over two decades.**

---

 # 4\. And we now have an unusually good audit mechanism

 The 400 CMR 9 regulations require a certified data center to file an **annual report**.

 The report must state:

 - actual jobs;
- actual investment;
- actual exempt expenditures.

 The owner's representative signs it under **pains and penalties of perjury**. EOED can also require accountant-certified cost accounting.  Massachusetts Government+1

 Even more importantly, the regulation says applications and associated materials are **public records** and may be subject to disclosure under Massachusetts public-records law.  Massachusetts Government

 So we have a very concrete records target:

 > **Every certified facility → application → anticipated investment → anticipated jobs → annual actual investment → annual exempt spending → actual jobs.**

 That is the ledger.

---

 # 5\. There is an important loophole/question hiding in the rules

 The regulation says applicants **may voluntarily provide**:

 - peak electric demand;
- annual electricity consumption;
- load-ramping periods;
- utility confirmation of grid capacity;
- commitments to pay for grid improvements;
- clean-generation commitments.

  Massachusetts Government  Notice the wording:

 ### "may"

 The financial investment and job requirements are mandatory.

 The detailed energy information is described as voluntary in this portion of the certification process.

 That creates an investigative question:

 > **Can we obtain the voluntary information for every applicant, and how often was it actually supplied?**

 If some applicants supplied detailed grid information and others didn't, that is useful context.

---

 # 6\. The state itself now recognizes the stranded-investment problem

 Healey's September 8 executive order says data centers can require substantial grid investment and specifically directs DPU to make sure **other ratepayers don't pay for distribution-grid upgrades or associated infrastructure needed for data-center demand**.  Massachusetts Government

 The June 25 framework similarly identifies:

 > large-load interconnection, stranded investments, and infrastructure

 as things that regulatory mechanisms should protect ratepayers from.  Massachusetts Government

 That gives us a much more defensible framing than "AI is extracting from people."

 The state itself has identified a potential mechanism:

 **private large load → infrastructure investment → stranded investment → risk to ratepayers.**

 Now we can test whether it actually happened.

 # 17\. And that's why I don't think "AI extraction" is the best starting hypothesis

 There's a more concrete mechanism:

 ### AI creates enormous demand for scarce infrastructure.

 That infrastructure includes:

 - electricity;
- transmission;
- substations;
- land;
- water;
- GPUs;
- construction;
- skilled labor;
- government permitting capacity.

 Whoever controls those bottlenecks can capture economic rents.

 That's a normal feature of industrial expansion.

 The public-interest question is:

 > **Are those rents being captured privately while infrastructure risks are socialized?**

 Massachusetts' own legislation and executive orders show that policymakers are sufficiently concerned about this possibility that they're designing rules specifically to prevent it.  Massachusetts Government+2

 That is considerably stronger evidence than speculation about a hidden network.

 ## The first thing that jumps out

 The BIS material is substantially more interesting than the earlier description suggested.

 There are actually **multiple BIS publications** that need to be connected:

 - **BIS Bulletin 137, “Circular relationships among AI firms,” October 1, 2026.** It analyzed AI investment deals from 2021–2025 and found that **28.7% of AI firms' investment deals by value involved another AI firm**, while **55.2% of incoming investment into AI firms came from other AI firms**. More strikingly, **46.4% of the value of AI-to-AI investment deals also involved commercial supply-chain relationships between investor and target.** BIS explicitly says these relationships increase opacity and create macroeconomic risks.  Bank for International Settlements
- **BIS Working Paper 1367, “The AI investment race,” July 14, 2026.** Its model estimates that the AI investment boom could represent **roughly 1.5× the socially efficient investment level under its baseline**, rising to about **3× where demand is less elastic**. It specifically models debt and circular equity ties and finds that a failure of one firm can propagate through the network.  Bank for International Settlements
- **BIS Annual Economic Report 2026** explicitly describes circular financing as hyperscalers/chipmakers taking equity positions in AI labs or neoclouds while those companies make purchase commitments back to the investors. BIS says the arrangements can obscure risk and potentially involve the same asset being pledged multiple times.  Bank for International Settlements+1   some risks—electricity infrastructure, stranded assets, tax expenditures, environmental costs—to parties outside the AI explicitly says debt and circular equity ties increase financial fragility, while its October bulletin documents the prevalence Entity B | Amount | Instrument | Cash/Noncash | Commitment | Guarantee | MW | GPU | Revenue

 That doesn't prove wrongdoing. But it **does validate the basic economic question we've been trying to investigate**: _how much of the apparent AI demand is ultimately being generated inside a tightly interconnected financing/supply chain?_

 And BIS is saying this independently.

 BIS — Circular relationships among AI firms

 BIS — The AI investment race

 # A3 is already producing something important

 The proposed CoreWeave question is particularly strong because we can now go directly to SEC filings.

 CoreWeave's **2025 10-K** says:

 - $60.7 billion of remaining performance obligations at December 31, 2025.
- About **67% of 2025 revenue came from Microsoft alone**.
- OpenAI committed up to approximately **$6.5B through May 2031** under one order.
- Meta initially committed approximately **$14.2B through 2031** under the 2025 order.
- More than **98% of 2025 revenue came from committed contracts**.
- Those contracts generally use take-or-pay structures.
- Customer prepayments were generally **15–25% of total contract value**.
- CoreWeave had **$21.6B of debt** at December 31, 2025.  SEC

 Then something happened in 2026 that changes the picture.

 On March 31, 2026, Meta entered a new order with CoreWeave for approximately **$21 billion**, extending capacity through December 2032. That's not a rumor—the agreement appears in CoreWeave's SEC filing.  SEC+1

 So the original mission's "$21B Meta agreement" was actually correct **for 2026**, but it wasn't correct if applied to the older 2025 filing.

 That's exactly why the mission's rule—

 > **date every finding; stale is worse than absent**

 —is essential.

---

 # There's an even more interesting CoreWeave number

 CoreWeave says its infrastructure is primarily financed through **asset-level debt supported by take-or-pay customer contracts**, supplemented by corporate debt and equity.  SEC

 And its filing says:

 > committed contracts accounted for over 98% of revenue in 2025.

 That's extremely useful for the ledger because we can construct a much more precise chain:

 **customer commitment → CoreWeave revenue visibility → debt capacity → data-center construction → GPUs → AI compute**

 rather than simply saying "CoreWeave has lots of debt."

 That's the sort of connection that can actually be tested.

---

 # A5 may be one of the biggest leads

 Oracle's own FY2026 disclosure is remarkable.

 Oracle says **$75 billion** of its large-scale AI contracts involve either:

  - customer prepayment for GPUs, or
- customers purchasing and supplying the GPUs themselves.

 Oracle explicitly says this reduces the amount of capital it needs to raise to construct AI data centers.  Oracle+1

 That's a huge clue for the financing map.

 It means we shouldn't simply model:

 **Oracle → AI infrastructure**

 We need to model:

 **Customer → cash/GPU → Oracle → data center → AI capacity → customer**

 The direction of capital can partially run **back toward the infrastructure provider from the customer**.

 Oracle separately says it raised **$43B of debt and $5B of equity during FY2026**.  SEC

 So A5 should absolutely stay in the investigation.

 But we should **not assume the $75B represents cash prepayments**. Oracle explicitly combines _cash prepayment_ and _customer-supplied GPUs_ into that figure. Those are economically different instruments.

 That's exactly where your proposed accounting rule becomes critical.

 # I would change A1 slightly

 I wouldn't immediately calculate "net financing" for Amazon, OpenAI, Anthropic, NVIDIA, etc.

 First we need **four separate ledgers**:

 ### 1\. Equity

 Examples:

 - Company A invests $X in Company B.

 # And there's a major new mission I'd add

 ## A7 — Follow the same dollar twice

 This may be the most important analytical test.

 For every major transaction, ask:

 **Did money or economic value from Company A eventually become revenue, financing, or collateral for Company B—and then flow back toward A?**

 For example:

 **NVIDIA → investment → AI company**

 then:

 **AI company → GPU purchases → NVIDIA**

 Or:

 **Microsoft/Amazon/Google → investment → AI lab**

 then:

 **AI lab → cloud commitment → Microsoft/Amazon/Google**

 Or:

 **AI company → prepayment → infrastructure provider**

 then:

 **infrastructure provider → GPU/data-center purchases → NVIDIA/other supplier**

 This is different from accusing anyone of circular accounting.

 It is simply tracing **economic circulation**.

 And BIS has now independently established that these circular relationships exist at meaningful scale.  Bank for International Settlements+1

 # One thing I would NOT do

 I wouldn't start with the assumption that the system is fraudulent, extractive, or deliberately engineered against ordinary citizens.

 Instead, I'd test increasingly strong hypotheses:

 ### Hypothesis 1

 AI investment is simply enormous because expected demand is enormous.

 ### Hypothesis 2

 AI investment is increasingly debt-financed because companies cannot fund the build-out from current cash flow.

 ### Hypothesis 3

 Companies are increasingly making reciprocal investments and commercial commitments, causing capital to circulate within the ecosystem.

 ### Hypothesis 4

 Those arrangements can make _forward demand_ look stronger than underlying end-user demand.

 ### Hypothesis 5

 The infrastructure build-out can transfer some risks—electricity infrastructure, stranded assets, tax expenditures, environmental costs—to parties outside the AI financing network.

 ### Hypothesis 6

 The system could become fragile if expected AI productivity/revenue fails to materialize.

 And importantly, **BIS itself is already investigating something very close to Hypotheses 2–4.** Its July paper explicitly says debt and circular equity ties increase financial fragility, while its October bulletin documents the prevalence of circular AI investment relationships.  Bank for International Settlements+1

 That doesn't establish Hypothesis 5. **That's where the Massachusetts evidence becomes crucial.**

---

 ## So yes: I'd build this.

 But I'd build it as a **forensic financial/physical-flow database**, not a narrative.

 The master schema I'd use is:

 | Date | Entity A | Relationship | Entity B | Amount | Instrument | Cash/Noncash | Commitment | Guarantee | MW | GPU | Revenue | Source | Primary? | Confidence |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |

Then every dollar gets an individual record.

 And every record gets a source.

 And **"not disclosed" becomes an actual finding rather than an empty cell.**

 The initial pass already tells us this is worth doing: CoreWeave's SEC filings, Oracle's $75B customer-funded/customer-supplied AI infrastructure, and BIS's independent finding of circular AI financing are three pieces of the same economic structure—but they are **not yet proof of a closed loop in any particular transaction**. That's the next thing the ledger needs to establish.  SEC+2

 If we continue, I'd prioritize **A2 → A3 → A5 → B1 → B2 → B5**, because those are the places where primary records can turn the broad theory into measurable dollar flows.

 # And I would add one major mission: "follow the dollar twice"

 then:

 It's simply a **flow-of-funds test**.

 And BIS's research gives us a legitimate reason to investigate it rather than dismissing it as a theoretical possibility.  Bank for International Settlements

---

 # One thing I would be particularly careful about

 The phrase **"circular financing"** can easily get turned into an accusation.

 There are at least four different possibilities:

 1. **Normal vertical integration** — companies invest in suppliers because they need capacity.
2. **Strategic financing** — an investor funds a customer/supplier to secure future business.
3. **Circular financial exposure** — companies increasingly depend on one another's balance sheets and contracts.
4. **Artificial demand / financial engineering** — transactions make demand or financial strength appear substantially greater than independent end-user economics justify.

 The first three can exist without fraud.

 The fourth requires much stronger evidence.

 So our job is to see **where the evidence stops**.

---

 # The most interesting eventual calculation

 I would like to get to this:

 ### For each major AI infrastructure node:

 **Committed future revenue**

 minus

 **required infrastructure spending**

 minus

 **debt service**

 minus

 **operating costs**

 = **economic cushion**

 Then run scenarios:

 - Microsoft revenue -25%
- OpenAI revenue/commitments -25%
- Meta commitment delayed
- GPU residual values fall
- electricity costs increase
- data-center construction delayed
- utilization below contract assumptions

 That tells us whether the system is actually fragile.

 And then we can ask:

 > **Who absorbs the loss?**

 Investors?

 Lenders?

 AI companies?

 Cloud customers?

 Equipment manufacturers?

 Utilities?

 Taxpayers?

 Ratepayers?

 That's much more informative than trying to decide beforehand whether the system is "good" or "bad."

---

 ## My assessment of where we are

 **The investigation has crossed an important threshold.**

 We now have independently documented evidence for:

 - substantial AI-to-AI investment;
- commercial relationships between AI investors and targets;
- very high customer concentration at CoreWeave;
- tens of billions in CoreWeave debt;
- multibillion-dollar long-term AI capacity commitments;
- customer-funded/customer-supplied AI infrastructure at Oracle;
- tens of billions of Oracle debt/equity raised to support infrastructure;
- an available BIS transaction dataset;
- an available ISO-NE load-forecast dataset.

 Those facts **do not establish a conspiracy or fraudulent system**.

 But they establish a real, measurable phenomenon:

 > **AI infrastructure is increasingly being built through a dense network in which capital, customers, suppliers, cloud capacity, GPUs, contracts and debt are interconnected.**

 And that is exactly the phenomenon we should now map.

 The next step I'd take is **not another narrative report**. I'd pull the **BIS Excel dataset and the underlying CoreWeave/Oracle filings**, construct the first transaction-level network, and identify the largest circular relationships. That's where we can move from "this looks interesting" to **names, dates, dollars, counterparties and actual flows**.

[Oct 05, 2026, 7:18 PM EDT] RICKY:

what else do you think we're missing here? 


Yes. I infrastructure contracts themselves incorporate customer financing through prepayments or customer-supplied GPUs. CoreWeave's filings show infrastructure borrowing secured’d treat this as the execution phase now—not another round of speculative theory.

 I pulled the highest-value primary documents first. A few of the earlier ledger claims are already looking **more solid than the surrounding narrative**, while a few numbers need to be corrected or carefully separated.

 ## 1\. The BIS material is real, and it is stronger than a press summary

 The  BIS Bulletin 137 — “Circular relationships among AI firms”  was published October 1, 2026, by Jon Frost, Rudrudh Kansal, Kumar Rishabh, Vatsala Shreeti and Leanne Si Ying Zhang.

 The actual BIS document says:

 - **28.7%** of AI firms' investment deals by deal value, 2021–2025, involved another AI firm as the target.
- **55.2%** of incoming AI investment, by value, came from other AI firms.
- Of AI-to-AI investment deals, **16.1% by deal count and 46.4% by deal value** also involved a commercial supply-chain relationship between investor and target.
- BIS explicitly says these circular relationships create **macrofinancial risks and increased opacity**.  Bank for International Settlements

 Most importantly, the BIS page actually provides the underlying **Excel dataset**, rather than merely presenting the graphs.  Bank for International Settlements

 I was able to identify the file, although the web reader cannot directly parse the XLSX. So I would mark **R3-Q1 as “dataset located; machine-readable contents not yet extracted,” not as a dead end.**

 That's an important distinction.

 ### What BIS does _not_ establish

 It does **not** establish that the companies are secretly manipulating markets, committing fraud, or deliberately creating a circular Ponzi-like structure.

 The BIS interpretation is much more defensible:

 > Some AI companies invest in other AI companies while simultaneously having commercial relationships with them.

 That is demonstrable.

 The investigative question is whether the resulting financial network makes the industry more fragile. That's where Working Paper 1367 becomes particularly important.

---

 # 2\. Working Paper 1367 is perhaps the most important document we've found

 The  BIS Working Paper 1367, “The AI investment race”  is also genuine.

 Its conclusion is considerably more interesting than simply saying “AI might be overvalued.”

 BIS models an AI investment race in which firms compete for dominant positions and finds:

 - baseline investment can reach approximately **1.5× the socially efficient level**;
- under less-elastic demand assumptions, the estimate rises toward **3×**;
- debt increases vulnerability because specialized AI hardware has poor liquidation economics;
- firms committing early can increase the likelihood and severity of a subsequent bust;
- failure of one firm can propagate through a concentrated network of financial exposures.  Bank for International Settlements

 This gives us a much better analytical framework than “AI bubble.”

 The actual hypothesis to test is:

 **Are enormous infrastructure commitments being made because expected end-user demand justifies them—or because financing, competition, contractual commitments and strategic positioning reinforce one another?**

 That is a testable question.

---

 # 3\. CoreWeave is already a very useful stress-test case

 This is where the investigation gets concrete.

 CoreWeave's 2025 10-K reports:

 - **$21.6 billion total indebtedness** at December 31, 2025.
- **$3.7 billion** of undrawn borrowing availability.
- **$9.7 billion** outstanding under delayed-draw term-loan facilities.
- **$5.2 billion** of OEM/software financing arrangements.
- **$6.4 billion** of notes issued during 2025.
- accumulated deficit of **$2.6 billion** at year-end 2025.
- substantial continuing infrastructure investment requirements.  SEC

 So one correction to the brief is necessary:

 **The $35.6B figure should not be treated as CoreWeave's December 31, 2025 debt balance.**

 The primary filing gives us **$21.6B total indebtedness** at that date. There may be later obligations, facilities, or a different definition behind the $35.6B figure, but we shouldn't put $35.6B in the ledger until we identify exactly what it represents.

 That's precisely the kind of correction the investigation needs.

---

 # 4\. The Microsoft concentration is extraordinary

 The 2025 10-K says approximately:

 **67% of CoreWeave's entire 2025 revenue came from Microsoft.**  SEC

 That's an enormous concentration.

 And there is an interesting temporal transition.

 CoreWeave's earlier filings show Microsoft representing:

 - **71%** of revenue in Q2 2025;
- **67%** in Q3 2025;
- approximately **67% for the full year 2025**.  SEC+2

 So the Microsoft relationship isn't a trivial historical customer relationship.

 It is a central component of CoreWeave's business model.

---

 # 5\. Then the other side of the network starts appearing

 CoreWeave's 2026 filing says:

 - In May 2025, CoreWeave entered an MSA with OpenAI.
- In September 2025, OpenAI committed to pay CoreWeave **up to approximately $6.5B through May 31, 2031**.
- In March 2026, Meta committed approximately **$21B** to CoreWeave through December 2032, including additional capacity.  SEC

 And the underlying SEC filing makes the Meta arrangement particularly clear:

 Meta is buying **reserved AI cloud-computing capacity** from CoreWeave.

 The $21B is not an equity investment in CoreWeave. It is a **customer commitment / commercial contract**.

 That's important for the ledger.

 So we now have:

 | Entity | Relationship to CoreWeave | Amount | Instrument |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Microsoft | Customer | \~67% of 2025 revenue | Commercial revenue |
| OpenAI | Customer | up to \~$6.5B | Contractual commitment |
| Meta | Customer | \~$21B | Contractual commitment |
| Lenders | Financing providers | billions | Debt |
| CoreWeave | Infrastructure operator | — | Borrower + service provider |

That is much more informative than simply saying “CoreWeave has $X billion of debt.”

---

 # 6\. And Meta's $21B isn't hypothetical reporting

 We have the actual SEC filing.

 CoreWeave's April 9, 2026 filing states that Meta committed approximately **$21B**, with access to new computing capacity through December 20, 2032 and an existing option extending to April 2032.  SEC

 The accompanying SEC-filed announcement says the capacity is intended for **Meta AI operations**, including inference workloads, and that deployments will include NVIDIA Vera Rubin systems.  SEC

 So that ledger entry is **primary-source verified**.

---

 # 7\. Oracle is another extremely interesting node—but the $75B must not be treated as cash

 This is one of the most important corrections in the entire investigation.

 Oracle's SEC filing says its **$75B** figure consists of large AI contracts where:

 - the customer **prepaid Oracle for GPUs**, **or**
- the customer **purchased and supplied GPUs to Oracle**.

 Oracle explicitly says these two categories together totaled **$75B**.  SEC

 Therefore:

 **$75B ≠ $75B cash financing.**

 It is a mixed category.

 We must split:

 1. customer cash prepayments;
2. customer-supplied hardware;
3. ordinary contractual RPO.

 Otherwise we're manufacturing a financing number that Oracle itself did not report.

---

 # 8\. But Oracle gave us another number that is much cleaner

 Oracle's 2026 10-K separately reports:

 **$4.6B of customer prepayments during fiscal 2026 that contained a significant financing component.**

 That's actual accounting disclosure—not RPO, not a contract ceiling, and not customer-supplied GPUs.  SEC

 So the ledger should contain at least three distinct Oracle entries:

 | Oracle figure | What it actually represents |
| --- | --- |
| $75B | AI contracts involving customer prepayment **or customer-supplied GPUs** |
| $4.6B | Customer prepayments containing significant financing components during FY2026 |
| $638B | Total remaining performance obligations at May 31, 2026 |

Those numbers **must not be added together**.

 This validates one of the other AI's central methodological rules.

---

 # 9\. The really interesting pattern is emerging

 At this point I would **not** call this a conspiracy.

 I would call it a **financial-industrial network with unusually tight vertical and horizontal dependencies**.

 Consider the relationships we're able to document:

 **AI labs → cloud providers → infrastructure companies → GPU vendors → capital markets → AI labs**

 while simultaneously:

 **AI companies invest in AI companies**

 and:

 **AI companies become customers of infrastructure companies**

 and:

 **infrastructure companies borrow against contracted future cash flows**

 and:

 **customers sometimes provide the capital or hardware necessary to construct the infrastructure**

 That is exactly the sort of structure BIS is studying.

 The important question is whether this network is merely efficient specialization—or whether the interconnectedness is causing **capital expenditures, valuations and debt capacity to reinforce one another faster than underlying end-user demand can justify**.

 BIS's Working Paper 1367 explicitly models that possibility.  Bank for International Settlements

---

 # 10\. There's an especially important CoreWeave feedback loop to test

 CoreWeave says its delayed-draw facilities are collateralized by assets underlying contributed contracts and pledged contractual cash flows. The loans are drawn as infrastructure is built to support customer requirements and amortize as contracted cash flows are generated.  SEC

 That gives us a structure worth diagramming:

 **Customer commitment**

 ↓

 **CoreWeave obtains financing**

 ↓

 **CoreWeave buys/builds GPU infrastructure**

 ↓

 **Infrastructure produces contracted compute capacity**

 ↓

 **Customer payments generate cash flow**

 ↓

 **Cash flow services debt**

 ↓

 **Debt capacity permits additional infrastructure**

 ↓

 **Additional customer contracts justify additional borrowing**

 That's not inherently bad.

 It's a perfectly legitimate project-finance mechanism.

 But it means the crucial stress-test isn't simply:

 > "Does AI make money?"

 It's:

 > **What happens when several links in the chain weaken simultaneously?**

 That's exactly where R4-Q1 becomes valuable.

---

 # 11\. We can now define the CoreWeave stress test properly

 The primary data gives us the ingredients.

 We should calculate:

 **Contracted revenue**

 minus

 **required infrastructure investment**

 minus

 **interest/principal requirements**

 minus

 **operating costs**

 = **economic cushion**

 Then run:

 - Microsoft revenue −25%
- Meta deployment delayed
- OpenAI deployment delayed
- GPU residual value −30%
- utilization below contracted level
- refinancing unavailable
- customer default
- multiple shocks simultaneously

 The critical point is that **contracted revenue is not the same thing as cash immediately available to service debt**.

 We need contract duration, payment schedule, cancellation provisions, prepayments, collateral arrangements, capex requirements and debt maturities.

 That is the next serious calculation.

---

 # 12\. The BIS Excel file is now a priority rather than a vague lead

 The BIS page explicitly provides the Excel file underlying Bulletin 137's graphs.  Bank for International Settlements

 The web interface identifies the file as:

 `circular-relationships-among-ai-firms-data.xlsx`

 The browser couldn't parse the XLSX itself, so I won't pretend I've analyzed its rows.

 But we now know **the underlying dataset exists and is publicly linked by BIS**.

 That means R3-Q1 has changed from:

 > "Can we find the data?"

 to:

 > **"Can we extract and analyze the actual BIS dataset?"**

 That's a major improvement.

---

 # 13\. One thing I would change in the investigation

 I would add a new ledger field:

 ### **Economic direction**

 Every transaction gets classified as one of:

 - cash investment
- equity investment
- debt
- guarantee
- customer commitment
- prepaid customer cash
- customer-supplied equipment
- ordinary revenue
- tax expenditure
- government subsidy
- infrastructure expenditure
- contractual obligation

 And separately:

 ### **Economic dependency**

 - supplier dependency
- customer dependency
- financing dependency
- collateral dependency
- counterparty dependency
- regulatory dependency

 That prevents us from making the biggest analytical mistake: treating a $21B customer commitment, $21B debt facility, $21B investment, and $21B cash payment as economically equivalent.

 They aren't.

---

 # 14\. What is genuinely sticking out so far?

 Three things.

 ### 1\. Concentration

 CoreWeave's **67% Microsoft revenue concentration** is an objectively significant dependency.  SEC

 ### 2\. Interconnection

 BIS independently identifies a measurable phenomenon where AI companies invest in other AI companies while maintaining commercial supply-chain relationships. **46.4% of AI-to-AI investment deal value** had such a commercial relationship in its 2021–2025 dataset.  Bank for International Settlements

 ### 3\. Financing is embedded in the commercial architecture

 Oracle's disclosures demonstrate that some AI infrastructure contracts themselves incorporate customer financing through prepayments or customer-supplied GPUs. CoreWeave's filings show infrastructure borrowing secured by contracted cash flows.  SEC+1

 Those three observations reinforce one another.

 But they **do not yet prove** that the system is fraudulent, manipulated, or destined to collapse.

 The next question is whether the dependencies are large enough that a demand shock propagates through the network.

 That's the point where this stops being a collection of suspicious-looking transactions and becomes an empirical financial-stability investigation.

 ## The next three things I'd pull

 1. **BIS XLSX:** identify the actual firms/deals behind the 55.2% and 46.4%.
2. **CoreWeave debt holders + maturities:** identify exactly who is exposed to the $21.6B debt and when it comes due.
3. **CoreWeave contracts:** build Microsoft/OpenAI/Meta contracted revenue against capex and debt service.

 Those three together can tell us considerably more than another hundred news articles. And importantly, they give us the ability to discover that the hypothesis is **wrong** if the numbers don't support it.
